We conduct experiments on a broad range of four public benchmarks, including one real-world benchmark, a benchmark from PB competition, an integer linear programming optimization benchmark, and a crafted combinatorial benchmark, to evaluate the performance improvement achieved by AutoPBO and compare it with six state-of-the-art competitors, including two local search PBO solvers NuPBO and OraSLS, two complete PB solvers PBO-IHS and RoundingSat, and two mixed integer programming (MIP) solvers Gurobi and SCIP. AutoPBO demonstrates significant improvements over previous local search approaches, while maintaining competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art competitors. The results suggest that AutoPBO offers a promising approach to automating local search solver design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AutoPBO: LLM-powered Optimization for Local Search PBO Solvers."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoPBO, a framework that uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically enhance local search solvers for Pseudo-Boolean Optimization (PBO) problems. AutoPBO uses a multi-agent system and a greedy search strategy to iteratively improve the heuristic functions within a structured local search PBO solver (StructPBO) designed by the authors.  It addresses the problem of manually tuning heuristics in local search solvers, which often requires significant expert effort. The paper presents experimental results on four public benchmarks, demonstrating that AutoPBO can significantly improve the performance of local search solvers compared to their original versions and shows competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art solvers, including complete solvers and MIP solvers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The application of LLMs to automatically optimize *local search* algorithms for PBO, especially the modular approach, is a key aspect of the work. While LLMs have been used for algorithm design and automated heuristic generation in other areas like evolutionary algorithms and SAT solvers, this paper is one of the first to tackle PBO with this approach, especially focusing on improving existing solvers rather than from-scratch design.

A point of concern is the novelty of the *specific techniques used*. The architecture relies on a structuralized local search and a greedy optimization. While these methods might be novel in their application to PBO optimization with LLMs, they're not inherently groundbreaking on their own.  It should be noted that the authors contribute StructPBO, which enables LLMs to more effectively comprehend and optimize the PBO problem.

**Significance:**

The significance lies in the potential to automate the tedious and expert-intensive process of tuning heuristics in local search solvers. PBO is a powerful framework for modeling combinatorial problems, and better solvers have broad applications. If AutoPBO can consistently improve solver performance across a range of problems, it could have a noticeable impact on how PBO problems are tackled. The result highlights a viable path to automating algorithmic design in the constrained optimization space using LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a practical problem: Automating heuristic tuning.
    *   Demonstrates empirical improvements: AutoPBO consistently improves performance on several benchmarks and datasets.
    *   Introduces a structured approach: The StructPBO solver facilitates the application of LLMs.
    *   Clear Methodology: The multi-agent framework is well defined.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited LLM Exploration: The paper utilizes one LLM. Investigating the impact of different LLMs on the performance of the framework would strengthen the work.
    *   Generality: While the modular design enables isolated function optimization, the framework still caters to the PBO domain. Broader explorations into other optimization problems would establish the approach's versatility.
    *   Heuristic Dependence: The initial performance gains are constrained by the LLM's comprehension of the original solver. Further enhancements, such as RAG integration to aid LLM's comprehension, would improve the framework.
    *   Limited theoretical analysis: More theoretical exploration of *why* certain heuristic modifications are effective would increase the paper's value.
    *   Reliance on Benchmarks: The significance heavily relies on performance gains on standard benchmarks. While important, benchmarks can be limiting. Demonstrating how AutoPBO can lead to genuinely *new* insights into solving PBO would be valuable.

**Overall:**

AutoPBO presents a promising step towards automating the design and optimization of local search solvers for PBO, offering a valuable contribution to the field of combinatorial optimization. This research opens doors for future innovation by highlighting the potential of LLMs to advance optimization techniques for a wide range of problems. However, further expansion and development is required to tackle existing restrictions and establish a more profound influence.

### Detecting Regional Spurious Correlations in Vision Transformers via Token Discarding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04009v1)
- **Authors**: Solha Kang, Esla Timothy Anzaku, Wesley De Neve, Arnout Van Messem, Joris Vankerschaver, Francois Rameau, Utku Ozbulak
- **Abstract**: Due to their powerful feature association capabilities, neural network-based computer vision models have the ability to detect and exploit unintended patterns within the data, potentially leading to correct predictions based on incorrect or unintended but statistically relevant signals. These clues may vary from simple color aberrations to small texts within the image. In situations where these unintended signals align with the predictive task, models can mistakenly link these features with the task and rely on them for making predictions. This phenomenon is referred to as spurious correlations, where patterns appear to be associated with the task but are actually coincidental. As a result, detection and mitigation of spurious correlations have become crucial tasks for building trustworthy, reliable, and generalizable machine learning models. In this work, we present a novel method to detect spurious correlations in vision transformers, a type of neural network architecture that gained significant popularity in recent years. Using both supervised and self-supervised trained models, we present large-scale experiments on the ImageNet dataset demonstrating the ability of the proposed method to identify spurious correlations. We also find that, even if the same architecture is used, the training methodology has a significant impact on the model's reliance on spurious correlations. Furthermore, we show that certain classes in the ImageNet dataset contain spurious signals that are easily detected by the models and discuss the underlying reasons for those spurious signals. In light of our findings, we provide an exhaustive list of the aforementioned images and call for caution in their use in future research efforts. Lastly, we present a case study investigating spurious signals in invasive breast mass classification, grounding our work in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel method for detecting spurious correlations in vision transformers (ViTs) using a token discarding approach. The method identifies influential tokens (those most affecting prediction confidence when removed) and compares their location to object bounding box annotations.  Two metrics, Average Token Spuriosity Index (A-TSI) and Maximum Token Spuriosity Index (M-TSI), are proposed to quantify the degree to which influential tokens are outside the bounding box (i.e., associated with spurious correlations).  The authors conduct experiments on ImageNet using supervised, DINO, and MAE-trained ViTs, revealing that training methodology significantly impacts the reliance on spurious correlations.  They also identify problematic ImageNet classes and demonstrate the method's applicability in a real-world breast cancer detection scenario.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the application of token discarding, an intrinsic property of transformers, to explicitly identify and quantify spurious correlations *without* relying on traditional interpretability techniques like GradCAM. Using A-TSI and M-TSI to measure the degree of spurious correlations with explicit object of interest information is also a novel, quantifiable measurement. While some studies have used token discarding for other purposes (e.g., robustness, efficiency), its specific application here for *detecting spurious correlations in relation to ground truth bounding boxes* is a unique contribution.
* **Significance:** The work addresses a crucial issue in machine learning: the vulnerability of models to learning unintended patterns that undermine generalization. The method provides a practical tool for evaluating the trustworthiness of ViTs and identifying problematic data. The findings on how training methodology (supervised vs. self-supervised) affects spurious correlation reliance are valuable for guiding future research. The real-world case study on breast cancer detection highlights the practical relevance of the approach in safety-critical domains. The identification of problematic ImageNet classes and the public availability of these curated examples is another valuable contribution.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Principled Approach:** Leverages the intrinsic properties of ViTs (token discarding) for a more robust and less assumption-laden approach than reliance on interpretability methods that are open to debate.
    * **Quantifiable Metrics:** Introduces A-TSI and M-TSI, providing measurable assessments of spurious correlations.
    * **Comprehensive Experiments:**  Experiments are well-designed and performed on a large dataset (ImageNet) with various training methods (supervised, DINO, MAE).
    * **Real-World Application:** Demonstrates the applicability of the method to a practical medical imaging problem.
    * **Reproducibility:** Provides curated list of problematic images and makes the code available.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Annotations:**  The method, as presented, requires bounding box annotations, limiting its applicability in scenarios where such annotations are unavailable or costly to obtain. The authors attempt to alleviate this limitation by exploring attention maps as a substitute, but the results indicate this substitution is not consistently reliable across architectures.
    * **Computational Cost:** Token discarding involves multiple forward passes, which can be computationally expensive, especially for larger models or datasets. While attention maps could potentially alleviate this, the results so far point towards attention-maps having variable effectiveness in the quality of the results.
    * **Limited Analysis of Attention Maps:** The analysis of attention maps as annotation substitutes is limited, and the results show weak correlation with token influence maps for some models. Deeper investigation is needed.
    * **Metric Sensitivity:** While the authors offer the distinction between A-TSI and M-TSI, a more detailed sensitivity analysis (e.g., on smaller shifts in relative location of important tokens, on object size) could strengthen the guidance on which metric is best in different situations.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper introduces a genuinely novel approach with demonstrated value in a critical area.  The strengths of the methodology and the experiments are substantial. The weaknesses relate primarily to practical limitations (annotation requirements, computational cost) and avenues for future improvement, rather than fundamental flaws in the concept. While annotation-free analysis could be improved, the exploration of attention-map substitutes is a welcome approach. Given the novelty, rigorous evaluation, and identified actionable classes in ImageNet, the paper represents a significant contribution to the field, especially in building explainable and robust deep learning models.

### NER Retriever: Zero-Shot Named Entity Retrieval with Type-Aware Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04011v1)
- **Authors**: Or Shachar, Uri Katz, Yoav Goldberg, Oren Glickman
- **Abstract**: We present NER Retriever, a zero-shot retrieval framework for ad-hoc Named Entity Retrieval, a variant of Named Entity Recognition (NER), where the types of interest are not provided in advance, and a user-defined type description is used to retrieve documents mentioning entities of that type. Instead of relying on fixed schemas or fine-tuned models, our method builds on internal representations of large language models (LLMs) to embed both entity mentions and user-provided open-ended type descriptions into a shared semantic space. We show that internal representations, specifically the value vectors from mid-layer transformer blocks, encode fine-grained type information more effectively than commonly used top-layer embeddings. To refine these representations, we train a lightweight contrastive projection network that aligns type-compatible entities while separating unrelated types. The resulting entity embeddings are compact, type-aware, and well-suited for nearest-neighbor search. Evaluated on three benchmarks, NER Retriever significantly outperforms both lexical and dense sentence-level retrieval baselines. Our findings provide empirical support for representation selection within LLMs and demonstrate a practical solution for scalable, schema-free entity retrieval. The NER Retriever Codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/ShacharOr100/ner_retriever
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NER Retriever: Zero-Shot Named Entity Retrieval with Type-Aware Embeddings":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NER Retriever, a novel zero-shot framework for Named Entity Retrieval (NER). Unlike traditional NER systems that rely on pre-defined entity types and labeled data, NER Retriever addresses the ad-hoc NER task where entity types are user-defined at query time.  The core idea is to leverage the internal representations of pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) to embed both entity mentions and user-provided type descriptions into a shared semantic space.  The authors demonstrate that mid-layer transformer block representations within LLMs are more effective at capturing fine-grained type information than standard top-layer embeddings.  They then train a contrastive projection network to refine these representations, aligning type-compatible entities and separating unrelated ones.  This results in compact, type-aware embeddings suitable for nearest-neighbor search, enabling efficient retrieval of documents containing entities matching the user's specified type.  Experiments on several benchmarks show that NER Retriever outperforms both lexical and dense retrieval baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its approach to zero-shot ad-hoc NER using internal LLM representations, specifically emphasizing the importance of mid-layer transformer blocks. While using LLMs for NER is not new, the systematic exploration of different layers and components within the LLM architecture, coupled with the contrastive learning approach, represents a significant contribution. The idea of distilling this layer into a smaller vector representation is also novel. The comparison and justification for using the mid-layer value (V) vectors within the self-attention module is a well-reasoned contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to address a practical need in information retrieval and knowledge base construction: retrieving entities of user-defined types without requiring task-specific training or fixed schemas.  The results demonstrate a practical solution for scalable, schema-free entity retrieval. The findings also offer valuable insights into the internal workings of LLMs, highlighting the role of specific layers and components in encoding type-sensitive information. The fact that they are able to achieve this on multiple datasets strengthens its impact. The release of the codebase enhances the reproducibility and adoption of the method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of ad-hoc NER and motivates the need for a zero-shot retrieval approach.
    *   **Rigorous experimentation:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, with comparisons against strong baselines on multiple benchmarks. The ablation studies effectively isolate the contributions of different components of the system.
    *   **Insightful analysis:** The analysis of LLM layer selection and type sensitivity is compelling and provides valuable insights into the internal workings of these models.
    *   **Practical application:** The framework is designed for practical use, with a focus on efficiency and scalability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The approach is inherently dependent on the quality and characteristics of the underlying LLM. Performance may be limited in specialized domains where the LLM lacks sufficient knowledge. The success is tied to ongoing advancements in LLMs, making the presented approach vulnerable to architectural changes to these models.
    *   **Entity Detection phase:** The entity detection phase using the pre-trained model could introduce errors that propagate downstream. While the oracle span evaluation is a good way to show potential improvements, there could be limitations if the entity detection is not as effective. The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of error cases and limitations.
    *   **Limited Generalization beyond Text:** The proposed approach primarily focuses on textual data and its effectiveness in handling other modalities or complex data structures (e.g., knowledge graphs) is currently unclear.
    *   **BM25 comparison on NERetrieve**: The paper mentions BM25 performing competitively on the NERetrieve dataset due to the descriptive nature of the data. More analysis as to when BM25 would be preferred over the proposed method could provide a better understanding of the method's limitations.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in zero-shot NER, information retrieval, and knowledge base construction. The insights into LLM layer selection and the effectiveness of contrastive learning could inform the design of other entity-aware systems.  The framework provides a practical starting point for building scalable and flexible entity retrieval applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite some limitations, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of NER. The systematic exploration of LLM internals, the contrastive learning approach, and the thorough experimental evaluation demonstrate a clear advancement over existing methods. While the reliance on LLMs and the potential for error propagation are valid concerns, the benefits of the proposed framework outweigh these drawbacks. The paper has strong potential for influencing future research and practical applications.

### On Robustness and Reliability of Benchmark-Based Evaluation of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04013v1)
- **Authors**: Riccardo Lunardi, Vincenzo Della Mea, Stefano Mizzaro, Kevin Roitero
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) effectiveness is usually evaluated by means of benchmarks such as MMLU, ARC-C, or HellaSwag, where questions are presented in their original wording, thus in a fixed, standardized format. However, real-world applications involve linguistic variability, requiring models to maintain their effectiveness across diverse rewordings of the same question or query. In this study, we systematically assess the robustness of LLMs to paraphrased benchmark questions and investigate whether benchmark-based evaluations provide a reliable measure of model capabilities. We systematically generate various paraphrases of all the questions across six different common benchmarks, and measure the resulting variations in effectiveness of 34 state-of-the-art LLMs, of different size and effectiveness. Our findings reveal that while LLM rankings remain relatively stable across paraphrased inputs, absolute effectiveness scores change, and decline significantly. This suggests that LLMs struggle with linguistic variability, raising concerns about their generalization abilities and evaluation methodologies. Furthermore, the observed performance drop challenges the reliability of benchmark-based evaluations, indicating that high benchmark scores may not fully capture a model's robustness to real-world input variations. We discuss the implications of these findings for LLM evaluation methodologies, emphasizing the need for robustness-aware benchmarks that better reflect practical deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "On Robustness and Reliability of Benchmark-Based Evaluation of LLMs".

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness and reliability of using benchmark-based evaluations (like MMLU, ARC-C, HellaSwag) for assessing Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors hypothesize that because these benchmarks present questions in a fixed, standardized format, they may not accurately reflect real-world scenarios where linguistic variability is common.  To test this, they systematically paraphrase questions from six common benchmarks, evaluate 34 state-of-the-art LLMs on both the original and paraphrased questions, and analyze the resulting changes in performance. The key findings are: 1) While model rankings tend to remain stable across paraphrased inputs, absolute effectiveness scores decrease significantly when questions are reworded. This suggests that LLMs struggle with linguistic variation. 2) Model consistency (giving the same answer across paraphrases) is not always a reliable indicator of correctness, especially for weaker models.  3) Older benchmarks may be more susceptible to overfitting or data contamination, leading to inflated accuracy on the original questions, which is then disrupted by paraphrasing. The authors conclude that current benchmarking methodologies may overestimate the true generalization abilities of LLMs and advocate for robustness-aware evaluation frameworks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem & Motivation:** The paper addresses a critical and timely issue: the limitations of current LLM evaluation methods.  The motivation is clear, as the reliance on static benchmarks could lead to an overestimation of real-world performance.
    *   **Systematic Methodology:** The approach is rigorous and well-structured. The systematic generation of paraphrases, coupled with the evaluation of a diverse set of LLMs, provides a strong empirical foundation. The use of GPT-4o for paraphrasing is reasonable given the scale, but the validation is an important element that the authors consider.
    *   **Clear Findings & Implications:** The findings are clearly presented and have important implications for the field. The observation that absolute performance drops with paraphrasing, despite relatively stable rankings, is significant. This points to the need for more robust evaluation metrics.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper explores different facets of the problem, analyzing consistency, accuracy, and the influence of benchmark age. The exploration of the relationship between model size, consistency, and accuracy is insightful.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide access to their data and code, which enhances the reproducibility of their work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Paraphrase Quality & Coverage:** While the automatic paraphrasing is validated, there is always a risk that the generated paraphrases may not fully capture the diversity and nuance of human linguistic variations. More discussion on the types of paraphrases generated and their similarity to real-world rephrasing could strengthen the analysis. The authors acknowledge this in their limitations.
    *   **Limited Reasoning Strategies:**  Focusing on zero-shot performance while acknowledging the potential benefits of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting (and discussing preliminary experiments with it) is a trade-off, but CoT could interact with the robustness to paraphrasing in interesting ways. While the motivation for focusing on zero-shot settings aligns with benchmark protocols, it may limit a deeper understanding of the interaction between paraphrasing and advanced reasoning abilities.
    *   **Benchmark Selection:** While the benchmarks chosen are common and cover a variety of tasks, there is limited discussion of whether these benchmarks are the most appropriate for evaluating specific aspects of robustness. Choosing different benchmarks may affect the results and conclusions.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Overall Conclusion:** While the paper's specific empirical analysis is novel, the general conclusion that LLMs are sensitive to input phrasing isn't entirely new. The key contribution lies in the systematic quantification of this sensitivity across various benchmarks and models.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a systematic and quantitative assessment of LLM robustness to paraphrased inputs across a wide range of benchmarks and models. While the general idea that LLMs are sensitive to phrasing is known, the thoroughness and breadth of this study are valuable. The findings highlighting the disconnect between ranking stability and absolute performance drops, and the potential for overfitting in older benchmarks, add significant nuance to the existing understanding.
    The work is also significant because it challenges the common assumption that high benchmark scores necessarily translate to robust real-world performance. The paper underscores the need for more sophisticated evaluation methodologies that account for linguistic variability and other real-world factors.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7** to this paper. Here's why:

*   The problem addressed is significant, the methodology is sound, and the findings are clearly presented. The results raise important questions about the reliability of current benchmark-based evaluations and motivate the development of more robust evaluation frameworks.
*   The paper is not groundbreaking in its overall conclusion (LLMs are sensitive to input phrasing), but the systematic empirical validation of this across multiple benchmarks and models is a valuable contribution. The analysis of consistency and the potential for overfitting add novelty.
*   The analysis could be improved by a deeper dive into the paraphrase generation process and its limitations, as well as the possible effects of more complex prompt strategies.
*   The paper contributes a clear step toward a deeper understanding of the problem, but is not yet a transformative advance in the field.

### CoT-Space: A Theoretical Framework for Internal Slow-Thinking via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04027v1)
- **Authors**: Zeyu Gan, Hao Yi, Yong Liu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has become a pivotal approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, a significant theoretical gap persists, as traditional token-level RL frameworks fail to align with the reasoning-level nature of complex, multi-step thought processes like Chain-of-Thought (CoT). To address this challenge, we introduce CoT-Space, a novel theoretical framework that recasts LLM reasoning from a discrete token-prediction task to an optimization process within a continuous, reasoning-level semantic space. By analyzing this process from both a noise perspective and a risk perspective, we demonstrate that the convergence to an optimal CoT length is a natural consequence of the fundamental trade-off between underfitting and overfitting. Furthermore, extensive experiments provide strong empirical validation for our theoretical findings. Our framework not only provides a coherent explanation for empirical phenomena such as overthinking but also offers a solid theoretical foundation to guide the future development of more effective and generalizable reasoning agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CoT-Space: A Theoretical Framework for Internal Slow-Thinking via Reinforcement Learning" introduces a novel theoretical framework for analyzing how Large Language Models (LLMs) learn to reason using Reinforcement Learning (RL) to generate Chains-of-Thought (CoT). It argues that traditional token-level RL formulations are misaligned with the reasoning-level nature of CoT. The paper proposes "CoT-Space," which recasts LLM reasoning as an optimization process within a continuous, reasoning-level semantic space, rather than a discrete token prediction task.  The paper analyzes CoT length convergence from noise and risk perspectives, highlighting the trade-off between underfitting and overfitting. Empirical experiments are presented to validate the theoretical claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the CoT-Space framework itself. Reframing LLM reasoning as an optimization within a continuous semantic space, rather than a discrete token-prediction process, represents a potentially significant shift in perspective. The analyses of CoT length convergence through the lenses of noise and risk, drawing parallels to concepts like sharpness-aware minimization and the bias-variance trade-off in traditional machine learning, are also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the theoretical understanding of how RL enhances reasoning in LLMs.  The "overthinking" phenomenon, and the generalization capabilities of reasoning models are important topics. The paper aims to provide a rigorous theoretical foundation for guiding future research in this area. However, the impact depends on whether the community embraces and builds upon the CoT-Space framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Theoretical Rigor:**  The paper employs a strong mathematical foundation, including formal definitions, theorems, and proofs, to support its claims.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The framework is applied to analyze both CoT length convergence and generalization capabilities, providing a holistic view of LLM reasoning.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Experiments are conducted to validate the theoretical predictions, providing empirical evidence for the framework's utility. The experiments are well-designed and use diverse datasets and models.
    *   **Addresses a Real Problem:** It targets the theoretical limitations in existing RL frameworks applied to LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The mathematical formalism can be dense and challenging for readers less familiar with theoretical machine learning. The paper could benefit from more intuitive explanations and illustrative examples.
    *   **Assumptions:**  The framework relies on certain assumptions, such as the exponential expressive redundancy of language. While justified, the sensitivity of the results to these assumptions should be discussed more thoroughly.
    *   **Direct Practical Impact:**  The paper is primarily theoretical. While it offers insights for designing more effective RL algorithms, the direct practical impact of the CoT-Space framework on LLM performance is not immediately clear.
    *   **Generalizability to more reasoning frameworks:** The framework is specifically tailored to CoT. It is not very clear whether the same framework is equally relevant or beneficial for other frameworks.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the CoT-Space framework is adopted and extended by other researchers, it could have a significant impact on the field. It provides a formal language for analyzing LLM reasoning and designing more effective RL algorithms. It could also inspire new regularization techniques or architectural innovations.

*   **Score Rationale:**

The paper is well-written and tackles a fundamental theoretical gap.  The mathematical rigor and novel perspective of CoT-Space are strengths. However, the high level of abstraction and the lack of direct, immediately applicable practical results temper its potential impact. The reliance on some assumptions also represents a limitation. While potentially influential, the framework's widespread adoption is uncertain. Overall, the paper presents a significant theoretical contribution that warrants a solid, but not exceptional, score.

### SMooGPT: Stylized Motion Generation using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04058v1)
- **Authors**: Lei Zhong, Yi Yang, Changjian Li
- **Abstract**: Stylized motion generation is actively studied in computer graphics, especially benefiting from the rapid advances in diffusion models. The goal of this task is to produce a novel motion respecting both the motion content and the desired motion style, e.g., ``walking in a loop like a Monkey''. Existing research attempts to address this problem via motion style transfer or conditional motion generation. They typically embed the motion style into a latent space and guide the motion implicitly in a latent space as well. Despite the progress, their methods suffer from low interpretability and control, limited generalization to new styles, and fail to produce motions other than ``walking'' due to the strong bias in the public stylization dataset. In this paper, we propose to solve the stylized motion generation problem from a new perspective of reasoning-composition-generation, based on our observations: i) human motion can often be effectively described using natural language in a body-part centric manner, ii) LLMs exhibit a strong ability to understand and reason about human motion, and iii) human motion has an inherently compositional nature, facilitating the new motion content or style generation via effective recomposing. We thus propose utilizing body-part text space as an intermediate representation, and present SMooGPT, a fine-tuned LLM, acting as a reasoner, composer, and generator when generating the desired stylized motion. Our method executes in the body-part text space with much higher interpretability, enabling fine-grained motion control, effectively resolving potential conflicts between motion content and style, and generalizes well to new styles thanks to the open-vocabulary ability of LLMs. Comprehensive experiments and evaluations, and a user perceptual study, demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, especially under the pure text-driven stylized motion generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SMooGPT: Stylized Motion Generation using Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper presents SMooGPT, a novel approach for generating stylized human motion using large language models (LLMs). The method leverages LLMs in a three-stage process: reasoning, composition, and generation.  First, the LLM (fine-tuned) acts as a "motion reasoner," translating motion content and style descriptions (text or motion sequences) into body-part-centric textual descriptions. Second, it acts as a "composer," merging these individual descriptions into unified, conflict-free descriptions reflecting both style and content. Third, it acts as a "motion generator," translating the composed body-part texts back into motion sequences. The key innovation is the use of body-part-centric textual descriptions as an intermediate representation, facilitating more interpretable and controllable stylization compared to existing methods that operate directly in latent spaces.  The authors fine-tune an LLM (Flan-T5) with pre- and post-training to enable these reasoning, composition, and generation capabilities. The approach is evaluated on HumanML3D and 100STYLE datasets, demonstrating improved performance, especially in pure text-driven stylization and generalization to new styles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has substantial novelty in several aspects.

    *   **Body-part centric representation:** The core idea of using body-part-level textual descriptions as an intermediate representation for stylized motion generation is novel and compelling. This approach offers more interpretability and control compared to existing latent space-based methods.
    *   **Reasoning-Composition-Generation framework:** The explicitly defined three-stage framework using the LLM's reasoning abilities is a clear and well-structured approach to tackle the problem. This framework provides modularity and allows for potential improvements in each stage.
    *   **LLM-based approach for stylized motion:** While LLMs have been used in motion generation before, the focus on stylized motion generation with this specific architectural design (reasoning, composing, generating body-part texts) is a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing the limitations of existing motion stylization methods, particularly the lack of interpretability, limited control, and difficulty in generalizing to new styles.

    *   **Improved interpretability and control:** The body-part-centric representation provides a more intuitive way to understand and manipulate the style of the generated motion.
    *   **Better generalization:** The open-vocabulary nature of LLMs enables better generalization to unseen styles compared to methods relying on curated style datasets or style encoders trained on limited data.
    *   **Text-driven stylization:** The method's strong performance in text-driven stylization makes it more accessible and versatile for users.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear and well-structured approach:** The paper clearly explains the method and its components. The motivation and design choices are well-justified.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The quantitative and qualitative results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach, especially in text-guided stylization. The ablation studies provide further insights into the importance of different components.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The use of multiple metrics (SRA, MM Dist, R-Precision, FS-Ratio) and a user study provides a comprehensive evaluation of the method's performance.
    *   **Handles conflicts effectively:** The explicit composition stage seems to handle the conflicts between content and style very well.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **VQ-VAE Bottleneck:** The VQ-VAE for body-part motion tokenization could be a bottleneck. The discrete nature and compression might lose fine-grained details, limiting the complexity of achievable motion dynamics. This limitation is explicitly acknowledged in the conclusion.
    *   **Reliance on ChatGPT for dataset creation:** The use of ChatGPT-3.5 for decomposing global descriptions into body-part-specific annotations introduces a potential bias and reliance on the quality of the LLM's output. While this is a practical approach given the lack of readily available data, it's a dependency that should be acknowledged. Error analysis or analysis of the dataset generated via ChatGPT would strengthen the results.
    *   **Computational cost:** The method involves fine-tuning a large LLM, which can be computationally expensive. Reporting the inference time is good, but more detail on the training costs (time, hardware) would be useful.
    *   **Limited motion dynamics:** The motion is relatively simple. It can be beneficial to try more complex motions like dance with rich styles to further demonstrate the robustness of the method.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in motion generation, stylization, and the application of LLMs in graphics. The body-part-centric representation and reasoning-composition-generation framework could inspire new approaches for controllable and interpretable motion synthesis. The successful use of LLMs for style transfer could also encourage further exploration of this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of motion stylization. The strengths of the paper lie in the clear approach, solid experimental results, and improved interpretability and control. The weaknesses are mainly related to the potential limitations of the VQ-VAE bottleneck and reliance on a closed-source LLM for dataset creation. However, the overall impact and novelty of the approach outweigh these limitations. The explicit acknowledgement of limitations is also seen as a positive quality of the paper.

### Synthesizing Sheet Music Problems for Evaluation and Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04059v1)
- **Authors**: Zhilin Wang, Zhe Yang, Yun Luo, Yafu Li, Haoran Zhang, Runzhe Zhan, Derek F. Wong, Jizhe Zhou, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Enhancing the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to interpret sheet music is a crucial step toward building AI musicians. However, current research lacks both evaluation benchmarks and training data for sheet music reasoning. To address this, we propose the idea of synthesizing sheet music problems grounded in music theory, which can serve both as evaluation benchmarks and as training data for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We introduce a data synthesis framework that generates verifiable sheet music questions in both textual and visual modalities, leading to the Synthetic Sheet Music Reasoning Benchmark (SSMR-Bench) and a complementary training set. Evaluation results on SSMR-Bench show the importance of models' reasoning abilities in interpreting sheet music. At the same time, the poor performance of Gemini 2.5-Pro highlights the challenges that MLLMs still face in interpreting sheet music in a visual format. By leveraging synthetic data for RLVR, Qwen3-8B-Base and Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct achieve improvements on the SSMR-Bench. Besides, the trained Qwen3-8B-Base surpasses GPT-4 in overall performance on MusicTheoryBench and achieves reasoning performance comparable to GPT-4 with the strategies of Role play and Chain-of-Thought. Notably, its performance on math problems also improves relative to the original Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, our results show that the enhanced reasoning ability can also facilitate music composition. In conclusion, we are the first to propose the idea of synthesizing sheet music problems based on music theory rules, and demonstrate its effectiveness not only in advancing model reasoning for sheet music understanding but also in unlocking new possibilities for AI-assisted music creation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to evaluating and training Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for sheet music understanding. The core idea is to programmatically synthesize sheet music problems based on music theory rules. This enables the creation of the Synthetic Sheet Music Reasoning Benchmark (SSMR-Bench) and a corresponding training dataset in both textual (ABC notation) and visual (rendered sheet music images) modalities.  The paper evaluates existing LLMs/MLLMs on SSMR-Bench, demonstrates the effectiveness of using the synthetic data for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to improve model performance on sheet music understanding and, furthermore, shows the potential for enhancing music composition. The paper demonstrates that models trained with this synthetic data also improve on existing music theory benchmarks and even, to some extent, on general math problems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the idea of synthesizing sheet music understanding problems *programmatically* based on established music theory rules. Previous benchmarks relied on human-designed questions or focused on specific aspects of music analysis (e.g., OMR).  The programmatic approach allows for scalable generation of verifiable questions, which is crucial for both evaluation and RLVR.  While the concept of data synthesis for LLM training is not entirely new, the application to sheet music and the systematic grounding in music theory are significant contributions.

*   **Significance:** Sheet music understanding is a critical enabler for AI musicians, but progress has been hampered by a lack of data and benchmarks. SSMR-Bench addresses this gap by providing a controlled and scalable evaluation platform. Demonstrating that RLVR with synthetic data can improve performance on this benchmark and transfer to existing benchmarks like MusicTheoryBench is a significant step forward. Showing that this training can *also* improve general reasoning abilities (demonstrated on Math) is a very valuable byproduct. The impact extends beyond just understanding sheet music. It opens up possibilities for AI-assisted music creation. The paper also clearly identifies a significant weakness of current MLLMs – the difficulty in interpreting visual sheet music compared to textual representations. This highlights a critical area for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined and executed methodology:** The data synthesis framework is clearly explained, and the experiments are comprehensive, evaluating a range of LLMs/MLLMs and RLVR techniques.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The evaluation includes both the newly created benchmark (SSMR-Bench) and existing benchmarks (MusicTheoryBench), providing a robust assessment of the proposed approach.
    *   **Focus on verifiable rewards:**  The use of synthetic data allows for the creation of problems with objectively verifiable answers, enabling the application of RLVR, which is an important advance in LLM training.
    *   **Clear identification of challenges:** The paper correctly highlights the challenges faced by MLLMs in interpreting visual sheet music and the limitations of the generated data.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplicity of Generated Questions:** The paper mentions in the discussion that the questions are relatively simple and can be solved by strong models like DeepSeek-R1. This limits the difficulty and discriminative power of the SSMR-Bench, and more complex, rule-based, verifiable problems are required. This impacts its longevity as a benchmark.
    *   **Limited Scope of Music Theory:** The paper focuses primarily on fundamental music theory concepts (rhythm, chords, intervals, scales). While important, real-world musical understanding involves more complex concepts (e.g., form, orchestration, expressive markings). A more comprehensive music theory foundation would be beneficial.

*   **Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field. It addresses a critical need (data and benchmarks for sheet music understanding) with a novel and well-executed approach. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of using synthetic data for RLVR and its potential to enhance both sheet music understanding and general reasoning abilities.  However, the simplicity of questions and limited scope of music theory incorporated into the benchmark are notable limitations, preventing a higher score. Also, while improving reasoning generally is promising, the impact there is not dramatic.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Arabic Chatbot Technologies in Education: An Overview
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04066v1)
- **Authors**: Hicham Bourhil, Yacine El Younoussi
- **Abstract**: The recent advancements in Artificial Intelligence (AI) in general, and in Natural Language Processing (NLP) in particular, and some of its applications such as chatbots, have led to their implementation in different domains like education, healthcare, tourism, and customer service. Since the COVID-19 pandemic, there has been an increasing interest in these digital technologies to allow and enhance remote access. In education, e-learning systems have been massively adopted worldwide. The emergence of Large Language Models (LLM) such as BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) and GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformers) made chatbots even more popular. In this study, we present a survey on existing Arabic chatbots in education and their different characteristics such as the adopted approaches, language variety, and metrics used to measure their performance. We were able to identified some research gaps when we discovered that, despite the success of chatbots in other languages such as English, only a few educational Arabic chatbots used modern techniques. Finally, we discuss future directions of research in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of Arabic chatbot technologies used in education. It highlights the increasing interest in these technologies, particularly since the COVID-19 pandemic. The authors review existing literature, identifying educational Arabic chatbots, categorizing them by approach (rule-based, retrieval-based, generation-based), language variety (Classical Arabic, Modern Standard Arabic, Dialects), and evaluation metrics.  The study reveals that educational Arabic chatbots are relatively scarce, primarily retrieval-based, and often evaluated using subjective, human-based metrics. The paper concludes by pointing out the need for more advanced (generation-based) chatbots utilizing standardized, automated metrics, and the importance of expanding Arabic corpora to support both Classical Arabic and dialects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevant Topic:** The topic is pertinent, given the growth of e-learning and the potential of chatbots in education. Focus on the Arabic language, which has a significant user base and specific challenges, makes the study targeted.
*   **Clear Structure:** The paper is well-structured, with a clear introduction, methodology, findings, and conclusion. The literature review identifies a gap in research regarding the use of advanced technologies in educational Arabic chatbots.
*   **Comprehensive Survey:** The authors comprehensively review existing literature to identify relevant Arabic chatbots, presenting a well-organized summary table outlining their characteristics.
*   **Identifies Gaps:** The paper accurately identifies key gaps in the field: the scarcity of educational Arabic chatbots, the dominance of retrieval-based approaches, the limited support for Classical Arabic and dialects, and the prevalence of subjective evaluation metrics.
*   **Future Directions:** The paper suggests future research directions, like leveraging deep learning, creating standardized metrics, and expanding Arabic corpora, which are helpful for further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the paper surveys existing chatbots, it doesn't provide in-depth analysis of their functionalities, limitations, or comparative performance. It's more of a descriptive overview than a critical analysis.
*   **Lack of Comparative Evaluation:** The paper doesn't compare the different chatbots to evaluate their strengths and weaknesses or identify the best practices and challenges in developing Arabic chatbots.
*   **Limited scope:** The paper does not mention any specific criteria for including or excluding studies in the review. While the search was performed until August 2024, the exact search terms used in databases are not mentioned.
*   **Generality of Recommendations:** The recommendations for future research are somewhat generic. For example, suggesting "leveraging deep learning" could be more specific by proposing particular architectures or techniques that are well-suited for the Arabic language.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in being, to the authors' knowledge, the first survey to focus specifically on *educational* Arabic chatbots. While previous reviews covered Arabic chatbots more broadly, the targeted focus on education provides a more specific and potentially impactful contribution.

The paper's significance is moderate. It helps to consolidate existing knowledge, identify research gaps, and suggest future directions. However, the lack of in-depth analysis and critical evaluation limits its potential impact on the field. It serves more as a starting point for further research than a groundbreaking contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's strengths in identifying a relevant and under-explored area, conducting a systematic survey, and highlighting important research gaps. However, the paper's limited depth of analysis, lack of comparative evaluation, and somewhat generic recommendations prevent it from achieving a higher score. The work is useful for researchers entering the field, but does not offer novel contributions to researchers already active in the space. The survey is well-constructed, but lacks the deeper insights and critical analysis that would elevate its impact.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### RepoDebug: Repository-Level Multi-Task and Multi-Language Debugging Evaluation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04078v1)
- **Authors**: Jingjing Liu, Zeming Liu, Zihao Cheng, Mengliang He, Xiaoming Shi, Yuhang Guo, Xiangrong Zhu, Yuanfang Guo, Yunhong Wang, Haifeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have exhibited significant proficiency in code debugging, especially in automatic program repair, which may substantially reduce the time consumption of developers and enhance their efficiency. Significant advancements in debugging datasets have been made to promote the development of code debugging. However, these datasets primarily focus on assessing the LLM's function-level code repair capabilities, neglecting the more complex and realistic repository-level scenarios, which leads to an incomplete understanding of the LLM's challenges in repository-level debugging. While several repository-level datasets have been proposed, they often suffer from limitations such as limited diversity of tasks, languages, and error types. To mitigate this challenge, this paper introduces RepoDebug, a multi-task and multi-language repository-level code debugging dataset with 22 subtypes of errors that supports 8 commonly used programming languages and 3 debugging tasks. Furthermore, we conduct evaluation experiments on 10 LLMs, where Claude 3.5 Sonnect, the best-performing model, still cannot perform well in repository-level debugging.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RepoDebug, a new multi-task and multi-language repository-level debugging dataset designed to evaluate the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). The dataset spans 8 programming languages and includes 3 tasks: Bug Identification, Bug Localization, and Automatic Program Repair. It meticulously constructs 22 distinct subtypes of bugs, classified into syntax errors, logic errors, reference errors, and multiple errors. The authors perform experiments on ten LLMs and show that even the best performing models have significant limitations in repository-level debugging.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in creating a comprehensive dataset tailored for evaluating repository-level debugging, a crucial aspect often neglected by existing benchmarks that focus on function-level code repair. The multi-task and multi-language nature of RepoDebug addresses limitations in existing datasets regarding diversity of tasks, languages, and error types. However, while the individual error types are common, the systematic classification and controlled injection using ASTs are a strong point.

*   **Significance:** Repository-level debugging is more realistic and complex than function-level debugging, making it more relevant to real-world software development challenges. By highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in this area, the paper underscores the need for further research and development in improving LLMs' debugging capabilities within realistic contexts. The paper offers a valuable resource for researchers to develop and test debugging models, which can substantially contribute to automatic program repair and improve software development efficiency. The dataset size is notable, with 30,696 instances, providing a substantial testbed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The dataset's breadth in terms of languages, tasks, and error types is a key strength.
    *   **Controlled Bug Injection:** The use of ASTs provides precise control over the location and nature of injected errors. This increases the dataset's reliability and allows for targeted evaluations.
    *   **Realism:**  The dataset construction leverages real GitHub repositories to capture realistic software engineering problems. Cross-file effects also enhance realism.
    *   **Evaluation:**  The paper provides a thorough evaluation of several closed-source and open-source models using appropriate metrics.
    *   **Analysis:** Thorough analysis is performed of error types, error numbers, and token length, and insights are derived on the weaknesses of current models and the challenges ahead.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Data Leakage:** The paper acknowledges potential data leakage issues. Although repositories created after 2022 are used, the results shows Claude 3.5 Sonnet performs worse on instances after April 2024 than before.
    *   **Error Injection:** Although the error injection method uses ASTs to provide preciseness, AST-based bugs injection can still potentially have artificial characteristics, which can lead to biases when evaluating LLMs.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Methods:** The evaluation metrics (BI, OBL, ABL, APR etc.) are existing metrics. The approach relies heavily on the dataset construction itself, rather than novel analytical methodologies.

*   **Potential Influence:** The RepoDebug dataset has the potential to become a standard benchmark for evaluating LLMs' debugging capabilities. It could foster research in more sophisticated debugging techniques and inform the development of more robust code repair systems.

*   **Overall:** While the paper does not introduce groundbreaking methodologies, its creation of a valuable new resource and its careful evaluation and analysis make a significant contribution to the field. The systematic approach to the bug injection and the realistic nature of the GitHub repositories strengthen the practical relevance of the work.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper's strength lies in the creation and validation of a high-quality, comprehensive dataset.  The lack of truly novel methods or analytics holds it back from being a higher-scoring paper. However, RepoDebug addresses a gap in the evaluation of LLM capabilities and its insights should be valuable in directing future research efforts.  The acknowledgement of potential limitations (data leakage, dependence on AST-based errors) shows the authors are aware of factors that could impact results and encourages critical consideration. This contribution justifies a score in the upper range.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Intermediate Languages Matter: Formal Languages and LLMs affect Neurosymbolic Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04083v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Beiser, David Penz, Nysret Musliu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) achieve astonishing results on a wide range of tasks. However, their formal reasoning ability still lags behind. A promising approach is Neurosymbolic LLM reasoning. It works by using LLMs as translators from natural to formal languages and symbolic solvers for deriving correct results. Still, the contributing factors to the success of Neurosymbolic LLM reasoning remain unclear. This paper demonstrates that one previously overlooked factor is the choice of the formal language. We introduce the intermediate language challenge: selecting a suitable formal language for neurosymbolic reasoning. By comparing four formal languages across three datasets and seven LLMs, we show that the choice of formal language affects both syntactic and semantic reasoning capabilities. We also discuss the varying effects across different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Intermediate Languages Matter: Formal Languages and LLMs affect Neurosymbolic Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of formal language choice on the performance of neurosymbolic reasoning systems that combine large language models (LLMs) with symbolic solvers. The authors introduce the "intermediate language challenge," which highlights the importance of selecting a suitable formal language for effectively translating natural language problems into a format that symbolic solvers can process. Through experiments with four different formal languages (Pyke, ASP, NLTK, and FOL) across three logical reasoning datasets and seven LLMs, the study demonstrates that the choice of formal language significantly affects both the syntactic and semantic reasoning capabilities of the overall system.  Furthermore, the effect of the language choice varies across different LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Important Problem Addressed:** The paper tackles a previously overlooked yet crucial aspect of neurosymbolic reasoning. While previous work has focused on the LLM translation component or the symbolic reasoning component, the choice of *intermediate language* has been relatively ignored.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The authors conduct a thorough empirical study, systematically comparing the performance of different formal languages across a variety of datasets and LLMs. The experimental setup appears rigorous and well-controlled.
    *   **Clear Contributions:** The paper provides clear and concrete findings that demonstrate the importance of formal language selection. The intermediate language challenge is well-defined and the results offer valuable insights into the factors that influence neurosymbolic reasoning success.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is clearly described, enabling reproducibility. This includes details of prompting techniques (CoT), formal languages used, and the specifics of the models and solvers.
    *   **Good Analysis:** The analysis of the results goes beyond just reporting numbers; the authors discuss specific types of errors (e.g., formatting errors in Pyke, negation errors in ASP) and analyze execution rate vs. accuracy.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Formal Languages:** While the study explores four formal languages, the scope could be broadened in future work. Considering other, perhaps more specialized or hybrid, formalisms might offer further insights.
    *   **Dependency on specific LLMs:** While the paper uses seven LLMs, the field is rapidly evolving. The conclusions drawn might be somewhat time-sensitive, as future generations of LLMs could potentially mitigate some of the differences observed. Generalizing the results to a broader LLM setting is desired.
    *   **Dataset Coverage:** While three datasets are used, these are still limited to specific logical reasoning tasks. The findings might not fully generalize to other domains that require different reasoning patterns or types of knowledge representation.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel perspective by explicitly focusing on the impact of intermediate language choice in neurosymbolic reasoning. Prior work has implicitly used formal languages but rarely justified or empirically evaluated the implications of this selection.
    *   **Significance:** The findings have practical implications for the design and development of neurosymbolic systems. By highlighting the importance of formal language, the paper guides researchers and practitioners in making informed decisions that can significantly improve reasoning performance. The identification of strengths and weaknesses of specific formal languages with particular LLMs allows users to tailor their implementation to specific tasks and hardware/software constraints.
    *   **Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research directions by encouraging a more systematic and informed approach to the design of neurosymbolic reasoning systems. It may lead to the development of new evaluation metrics and benchmarks that specifically address the intermediate language challenge.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

    The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of neurosymbolic reasoning by shedding light on the importance of intermediate language choice. The empirical validation is strong, the results are well-analyzed, and the findings have practical implications. While there are some limitations in scope, the paper's novelty and significance justify a relatively high score.

### Towards Stable and Personalised Profiles for Lexical Alignment in Spoken Human-Agent Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04104v1)
- **Authors**: Keara Schaaij, Roel Boumans, Tibor Bosse, Iris Hendrickx
- **Abstract**: Lexical alignment, where speakers start to use similar words across conversation, is known to contribute to successful communication. However, its implementation in conversational agents remains underexplored, particularly considering the recent advancements in large language models (LLMs). As a first step towards enabling lexical alignment in human-agent dialogue, this study draws on strategies for personalising conversational agents and investigates the construction of stable, personalised lexical profiles as a basis for lexical alignment. Specifically, we varied the amounts of transcribed spoken data used for construction as well as the number of items included in the profiles per part-of-speech (POS) category and evaluated profile performance across time using recall, coverage, and cosine similarity metrics. It was shown that smaller and more compact profiles, created after 10 min of transcribed speech containing 5 items for adjectives, 5 items for conjunctions, and 10 items for adverbs, nouns, pronouns, and verbs each, offered the best balance in both performance and data efficiency. In conclusion, this study offers practical insights into constructing stable, personalised lexical profiles, taking into account minimal data requirements, serving as a foundational step toward lexical alignment strategies in conversational agents.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Towards Stable and Personalised Profiles for Lexical Alignment in Spoken Human-Agent Dialogue" investigates the creation of stable, personalized lexical profiles for use in conversational agents, aiming to facilitate lexical alignment in human-agent interactions. The study varies the amount of transcribed spoken data and the size of lexical profiles (number of items per part-of-speech category) to determine an optimal configuration for representing an individual's speech patterns. The profiles are evaluated for stability and relevance over time using recall, coverage, and cosine similarity.  The results suggest that profiles created using 10 minutes of transcribed speech, with specific numbers of items for different POS categories (5 for adjectives/conjunctions, 10 for others), offer a good balance between performance and data efficiency. These profiles also showed reasonable generalisability to unseen data. The work contributes practical insights into building individualized lexical profiles that can support lexical alignment in conversational agents, particularly in contexts with limited real-time user input such as communication with people with dementia.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The novelty of the paper lies primarily in its practical approach to creating personalized lexical profiles specifically designed for supporting *lexical alignment* in human-agent dialogue, *particularly* in scenarios where user input is limited. While the *general* idea of lexical alignment and *personalization* is not new, the paper combines these two concepts in a novel way, addressing the need for systems robust to communication difficulties. The explicit focus on *stability* and *data efficiency* when constructing these profiles is also a valuable contribution. Much previous work in lexical alignment in human-agent dialogue has been based on either rule-based systems or relying on continuous user inputs.  The use of a static, pre-built profile to bootstrap lexical alignment (which could then be dynamically adjusted based on real-time conversation) is a sound design choice for the target use case. The use of LLMs to generate responses that are lexical aligned to the profile can potentially be a way for the agent to be more effective in such use cases.

**Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve human-agent interaction, particularly in contexts such as assistive technologies for individuals with cognitive impairments. By developing methods for creating stable and personalized lexical profiles, the research contributes to the development of more adaptive and user-friendly conversational agents.  The identification of an optimal data requirement and profile size provides practical guidance for building such systems. If such profiles can indeed improve the interaction quality and user perception in such situations, the research could be considered highly significant. The use case of dementia care is also relevant, as lexical alignment could help to maintain and enhance communication with individuals facing language difficulties. However, the paper lacks demonstration of this (i.e., a downstream application involving the proposed methods in a human-agent dialogue setting). This remains a major limitation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question and Objectives:** The paper clearly defines the research question and the two primary objectives.
*   **Systematic Methodology:**  The study uses a systematic methodology with well-defined experiments. The variations of data amounts and profile sizes allow for a comprehensive analysis.
*   **Relevant Evaluation Metrics:**  The use of recall, coverage, and cosine similarity is appropriate for evaluating the stability and relevance of lexical profiles.
*   **Practical Implications:**  The findings provide concrete guidelines for creating lexical profiles with a good balance of performance and data efficiency.
*   **Contextually Relevant Use Case:** The focus on supporting communication with individuals with dementia is a relevant and timely application.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Downstream Application:** The paper lacks a downstream evaluation demonstrating the *actual* impact of these lexical profiles in a real human-agent dialogue setting.  Without this, it is hard to assess the true significance of the work. Does using this profile in an LLM actually improve interaction quality?
*   **Limited Dataset:** The dataset used consists of interviews with Dutch veterans. While reflecting elderly speech, it may not be fully representative of all elderly individuals or specific populations, such as those with dementia. Further, the dataset is based on interview settings, which can be different than the scenarios where the model is envisioned to be deployed. The generalization performance of the model is, therefore, likely limited.
*   **Limited Demographic Diversity:** The predominantly male demographic in the dataset is another limitation that could affect the profiles' generalizability.
*   **No comparison to baseline:** The lack of comparison with standard alignment techniques is also a major shortcoming.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the design of conversational agents that are more adaptable to individual users' language patterns, especially in contexts where user input is limited or unreliable. It can inspire further research into personalized lexical alignment strategies and their impact on human-agent interaction. The work might also encourage the creation of more refined and context-aware profile update mechanisms.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the limitations in dataset, the lack of demonstrating the benefits of such profiles in a downstream application, and the lack of comparison to baseline, while acknowledging the paper's novel application to assistive technologies for the elderly, I assign a score of **5**.

*   The novelty is reasonable, providing a new perspective on profiling and personalization in situations where real-time conversation cannot be relied upon.
*   The significance is limited by the current state. The impact on downstream applications must be demonstrated.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### MEPG:Multi-Expert Planning and Generation for Compositionally-Rich Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04126v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Zhao, Liu Lin
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image diffusion models have achieved remarkable image quality, but they still struggle with complex, multiele ment prompts, and limited stylistic diversity. To address these limitations, we propose a Multi-Expert Planning and Gen eration Framework (MEPG) that synergistically integrates position- and style-aware large language models (LLMs) with spatial-semantic expert modules. The framework comprises two core components: (1) a Position-Style-Aware (PSA) module that utilizes a supervised fine-tuned LLM to decom pose input prompts into precise spatial coordinates and style encoded semantic instructions; and (2) a Multi-Expert Dif fusion (MED) module that implements cross-region genera tion through dynamic expert routing across both local regions and global areas. During the generation process for each lo cal region, specialized models (e.g., realism experts, styliza tion specialists) are selectively activated for each spatial par tition via attention-based gating mechanisms. The architec ture supports lightweight integration and replacement of ex pert models, providing strong extensibility. Additionally, an interactive interface enables real-time spatial layout editing and per-region style selection from a portfolio of experts. Ex periments show that MEPG significantly outperforms base line models with the same backbone in both image quality and style diversity.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "MEPG: Multi-Expert Planning and Generation for Compositionally-Rich Image Generation".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Multi-Expert Planning and Generation Framework (MEPG) designed to improve the generation of complex, multi-element images from text prompts.  MEPG addresses limitations in existing text-to-image diffusion models related to handling intricate prompts and achieving stylistic diversity. The framework consists of two main components:

1.  **Position-Style-Aware (PSA) Module:**  This module uses a fine-tuned large language model (LLM) to break down input prompts into precise spatial coordinates and style-encoded instructions.  Essentially, it plans the scene layout and style.

2.  **Multi-Expert Diffusion (MED) Module:** This module performs cross-region image generation using dynamic expert routing.  Different expert models (e.g., realism experts, stylization specialists) are selectively activated for different spatial regions via attention mechanisms. This allows for specialization in different parts of the image. The architecture supports lightweight integration and replacement of expert models. An interactive interface allows real-time spatial layout editing and style selection from a portfolio of experts.

The authors demonstrate through experiments that MEPG outperforms baseline models in image quality and style diversity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the synergistic integration of LLM-based spatial planning with a multi-expert diffusion model.  While LLMs and MoEs have been used in image generation before, the specific combination and the way they are applied here offer a unique approach. The PSA module for decomposing prompts into spatial and stylistic instructions is also a valuable contribution. The dynamic routing of experts based on attention mechanisms operating on spatial regions adds another layer of innovation.
*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up, the significance of the paper could be substantial.  Improving the compositional understanding and generation capabilities of text-to-image models is a crucial step towards more controllable and creative image synthesis.  The architecture's modularity and extensibility (allowing for easy addition of new expert models) are also significant strengths.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integration of LLM for spatial planning and expert diffusion is a well-motivated and potentially effective strategy.
    *   The modular architecture allows for easy experimentation with different expert models and diffusion backbones.
    *   The interactive interface enhances user control over image generation.
    *   The paper presents quantitative and qualitative results comparing MEPG to existing methods, demonstrating its superior performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper might lack sufficient detail on the specific training procedures for the PSA module and the gating mechanism in the MED module. This could hinder reproducibility.  The types of models used as the experts are also not described in sufficient detail.
    *   The reliance on a fine-tuned LLM in the PSA module could limit its generalizability to different types of prompts or domains.
    *   While the modularity of the architecture is highlighted, the practical challenges of managing and coordinating a large number of expert models could be underestimated.
    *   The paper mentions an interactive interface but lacks details on its implementation and capabilities.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper presents a promising approach with several innovative aspects, there are areas where it could be strengthened.  The reliance on specific training data and the potential challenges related to expert coordination could limit its widespread adoption. Without better descriptions of the modules' specific operations, the paper is harder to reproduce, and harder to properly asses its robustness. The paper demonstrates improvements over baselines but does not necessarily represent a paradigm shift in the field. Therefore, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
