The score of 7 acknowledges the paper's significant contributions in integrating LLM-based spatial planning with a multi-expert diffusion model and offering a modular and extensible architecture. However, it also reflects the concerns about the lack of details in the training procedures, the generalizability of the PSA module, and the potential challenges in managing a large number of expert models. While the paper improves upon existing methods, it is not a revolutionary advancement, justifying the assigned score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Technical Documents Retrieval for RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04139v1)
- **Authors**: Songjiang Lai, Tsun-Hin Cheung, Ka-Chun Fung, Kaiwen Xue, Kwan-Ho Lin, Yan-Ming Choi, Vincent Ng, Kin-Man Lam
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we introduce Technical-Embeddings, a novel framework designed to optimize semantic retrieval in technical documentation, with applications in both hardware and software development. Our approach addresses the challenges of understanding and retrieving complex technical content by leveraging the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). First, we enhance user queries by generating expanded representations that better capture user intent and improve dataset diversity, thereby enriching the fine-tuning process for embedding models. Second, we apply summary extraction techniques to encode essential contextual information, refining the representation of technical documents. To further enhance retrieval performance, we fine-tune a bi-encoder BERT model using soft prompting, incorporating separate learning parameters for queries and document context to capture fine-grained semantic nuances. We evaluate our approach on two public datasets, RAG-EDA and Rust-Docs-QA, demonstrating that Technical-Embeddings significantly outperforms baseline models in both precision and recall. Our findings highlight the effectiveness of integrating query expansion and contextual summarization to enhance information access and comprehension in technical domains. This work advances the state of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems, offering new avenues for efficient and accurate technical document retrieval in engineering and product development workflows.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've carefully reviewed the paper "Enhancing Technical Documents Retrieval for RAG." Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Technical-Embeddings, a novel framework for optimizing semantic retrieval in technical documentation within Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems. The framework addresses challenges related to understanding and retrieving complex technical content by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs). The key components of Technical-Embeddings include: (1) synthetic query generation using LLMs to diversify training data, (2) contextual summarization to extract essential information from technical documents, and (3) prompt tuning to specialize a pre-trained language model for the technical domain.  The framework is implemented using a dual-encoder architecture. The experiments, performed on RAG-EDA and Rust-Docs-QA datasets, demonstrate that Technical-Embeddings outperforms baseline models in terms of Mean Average Precision (MAP), Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR), precision, and recall. Ablation studies highlight the importance of each component.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in retrieving information from complex technical documents using existing RAG systems.
    *   **Novel Framework:** Technical-Embeddings presents a comprehensive and well-integrated framework that combines multiple techniques to address the problem. The combination of synthetic query generation, contextual summarization, and prompt tuning is a valuable contribution.
    *   **Solid Experimental Results:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence that Technical-Embeddings outperforms baseline models on two different datasets. The use of standard evaluation metrics (MAP, MRR, precision, recall) and ablation studies enhance the credibility of the results.
    *   **Well-structured and Written:** The paper is well-structured, clearly written, and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the *combination* of techniques is novel, the *individual* techniques (synthetic query generation, contextual summarization, and prompt tuning) are established. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of how the integration of these components specifically addresses the limitations of using them individually.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** Although two datasets are used, the RAG-EDA dataset is relatively small. More diverse and larger datasets would further strengthen the generalizability of the findings.  Also, both datasets still revolve around software development which narrows down the significance and generalizability of the finding for all tech documentation types.
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** While the paper compares against several baseline models, comparing against more recent and state-of-the-art RAG implementations (e.g., those using more sophisticated indexing or retrieval strategies) would increase the competitive positioning.
    *   **Scalability and Complexity:** The paper doesn't fully address the computational cost and scalability of the Technical-Embeddings framework, especially concerning the synthetic query generation and contextual summarization components. In technical documentation settings, where documents constantly evolve, the costs should be thoroughly described.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies in providing a practical and effective framework for improving information retrieval in technical domains. The results demonstrate that Technical-Embeddings can significantly enhance the accuracy and relevance of retrieved information, which can benefit professionals and researchers working with complex technical documents. It offers a valuable contribution to the field of RAG systems and provides a promising direction for future research.

*   **Novelty:**

    The novelty of the work is good, although it is not revolutionary. The *combination* of existing techniques into a cohesive framework specifically tailored for technical documentation retrieval is valuable.  It highlights the importance of tailoring general NLP techniques to specific domain characteristics.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7**.  The paper presents a well-executed study with strong empirical results demonstrating the effectiveness of a combined approach for improving technical document retrieval. The primary strengths are the practical framework and solid evaluation. The main weaknesses are the incremental nature of the novelty, the limitations of the datasets, and the lack of discussion about scalability. While the individual techniques are not groundbreaking, the integration and application within the specific context of technical documentation retrieval is a worthy contribution. The limitations concerning baselines, datasets, and scalability make a higher score unjustified, but it's still a valuable and methodologically sound piece of work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hyper Diffusion Avatars: Dynamic Human Avatar Generation using Network Weight Space Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04145v1)
- **Authors**: Dongliang Cao, Guoxing Sun, Marc Habermann, Florian Bernard
- **Abstract**: Creating human avatars is a highly desirable yet challenging task. Recent advancements in radiance field rendering have achieved unprecedented photorealism and real-time performance for personalized dynamic human avatars. However, these approaches are typically limited to person-specific rendering models trained on multi-view video data for a single individual, limiting their ability to generalize across different identities. On the other hand, generative approaches leveraging prior knowledge from pre-trained 2D diffusion models can produce cartoonish, static human avatars, which are animated through simple skeleton-based articulation. Therefore, the avatars generated by these methods suffer from lower rendering quality compared to person-specific rendering methods and fail to capture pose-dependent deformations such as cloth wrinkles. In this paper, we propose a novel approach that unites the strengths of person-specific rendering and diffusion-based generative modeling to enable dynamic human avatar generation with both high photorealism and realistic pose-dependent deformations. Our method follows a two-stage pipeline: first, we optimize a set of person-specific UNets, with each network representing a dynamic human avatar that captures intricate pose-dependent deformations. In the second stage, we train a hyper diffusion model over the optimized network weights. During inference, our method generates network weights for real-time, controllable rendering of dynamic human avatars. Using a large-scale, cross-identity, multi-view video dataset, we demonstrate that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art human avatar generation methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Hyper Diffusion Avatars: Dynamic Human Avatar Generation using Network Weight Space Diffusion":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for generating dynamic human avatars by combining person-specific rendering and diffusion-based generative modeling. The method operates in two stages: First, it optimizes a set of person-specific UNets, each representing a dynamic human avatar and capturing intricate pose-dependent deformations.  Second, it trains a hyper diffusion model over the optimized network weights of these UNets. This allows for generating new network weights at inference time, which can then be used to render controllable, dynamic human avatars in real-time using pose-dependent 3D Gaussian Splatting. The key idea is to learn a distribution over the network weights that represent different human avatars, enabling the generation of novel avatars with realistic pose-dependent deformations. The method leverages a large-scale, cross-identity, multi-view video dataset and claims to outperform existing human avatar generation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques:
*   **Network Weight Space Diffusion for Human Avatars:** While diffusion models in network weight space have been explored in other contexts (e.g., shape generation), applying it specifically to dynamic human avatar generation, capturing pose-dependent deformations, is a novel contribution. It addresses limitations of current diffusion-based human avatar generation techniques that struggle with realistic pose-dependent deformations.
*   **Unification of Person-Specific and Generative Approaches:** Bridging the gap between high-quality, person-specific rendering and the generalization capabilities of diffusion models is a significant step. Previous methods often prioritized one aspect at the expense of the other. The approach of learning a distribution over the weights of person-specific fine-tuned networks enables generalization without sacrificing per-identity detail learned during fine-tuning.

**Significance:**

The potential impact of this work is high, particularly for applications in:
*   **Real-Time Communication and Virtual Reality:** Generating realistic and controllable avatars is essential for immersive experiences.
*   **Gaming and Entertainment:** Enabling the creation of diverse and realistic characters is highly valuable.
*   **Improved Generalization:** Moving beyond person-specific models is crucial for scalability and wider applicability.
The paper contributes to a growing body of work on neural rendering and generative modeling for 3D humans, but adds a unique element.

**Strengths:**

*   **Combines Strengths of Different Approaches:** The method successfully merges the benefits of person-specific rendering with the generative power of diffusion models.
*   **Realistic Pose-Dependent Deformations:** The network weight space diffusion approach allows for modeling complex deformations, which is a significant improvement over simpler skeleton-based animation.
*   **Real-Time Rendering:** Using 3D Gaussian Splatting as the rendering engine allows for fast and interactive avatar manipulation.
*   **Good Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates, with quantitative and qualitative comparisons, that their method outperforms existing avatar generation methods on rendering quality and diversity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Multi-View Data:** The method still relies on a multi-view video dataset for training the initial UNets. While it aims to generalize across identities, the initial per-avatar training phase might still be data-intensive, and a key limitation would be performance on identities without such training data.
*   **Complexity:** The two-stage pipeline adds complexity to the overall system. Training both the UNets and the diffusion model requires significant computational resources.
*   **UNet Generalization to Novel Poses:** The paper acknowledges a limitation in the UNet's ability to generalize to unseen poses. This suggests a potential area for future improvement. This may also impact realism and stability of pose changes if the network is not trained with an extensive collection of poses.
*   **Limited details on UNet architecture:** Although there is a reduction of the number of hidden channels to 64 to reduce the number of trainable parameters (for the sake of diffusion model training), the paper lacks detail for how exactly this affects the capacity of the UNet.
*   **Lack of ablation on components:** While there is an ablation study in the choice of diffusion model (i.e. how to model the network weights), there isn't an explicit study conducted to determine how performance is affected by training, or not training the networks with both Puv and Nuv.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of dynamic human avatar generation. The unification of person-specific and generative approaches, along with the emphasis on realistic pose-dependent deformations, is a clear advancement over existing techniques. The use of network weight space diffusion is a unique and promising approach.

However, the method still has some limitations, particularly in terms of data requirements and generalization to novel poses. It also builds upon existing techniques like UNets and Gaussian Splatting, so it is not a completely revolutionary method. The complexity of the system is another factor to consider. Taking into account the strengths and weaknesses, the paper earns a **Score: 7**. It is a well-executed and innovative approach that addresses a challenging problem, but still has potential for further improvement and refinement. It is likely to inspire future research in the area of generative human avatars.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### TAGAL: Tabular Data Generation using Agentic LLM Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04152v1)
- **Authors**: Benoît Ronval, Pierre Dupont, Siegfried Nijssen
- **Abstract**: The generation of data is a common approach to improve the performance of machine learning tasks, among which is the training of models for classification. In this paper, we present TAGAL, a collection of methods able to generate synthetic tabular data using an agentic workflow. The methods leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) for an automatic and iterative process that uses feedback to improve the generated data without any further LLM training. The use of LLMs also allows for the addition of external knowledge in the generation process. We evaluate TAGAL across diverse datasets and different aspects of quality for the generated data. We look at the utility of downstream ML models, both by training classifiers on synthetic data only and by combining real and synthetic data. Moreover, we compare the similarities between the real and the generated data. We show that TAGAL is able to perform on par with state-of-the-art approaches that require LLM training and generally outperforms other training-free approaches. These findings highlight the potential of agentic workflow and open new directions for LLM-based data generation methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TAGAL, a collection of three training-free methods (SynthLoop, ReducedLoop, and Prompt-Refine) for generating synthetic tabular data using agentic Large Language Models (LLMs).  TAGAL leverages LLMs in an iterative workflow where the LLM generates data, another LLM analyzes the data and provides feedback, and the generation process is repeated based on this feedback. The methods aim to improve data quality and utility without requiring fine-tuning of the LLMs.  The paper evaluates TAGAL on several datasets, assessing the utility of the generated data for downstream classification tasks and the similarity between real and synthetic data using precision and recall metrics.  The results show that TAGAL can achieve performance comparable to or better than state-of-the-art training-based methods and consistently outperforms other training-free approaches. The paper also investigates the impact of different prompt variants and meta-parameters on the performance of TAGAL.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using an agentic workflow with LLMs for tabular data generation has *some* novelty, but its impact is less clear. Agentic workflows have been used in other domains (e.g., question answering), so the adaptation to tabular data generation is an incremental contribution.  The core of the novelty lies in the specific implementation of the feedback loop and the different methods proposed (SynthLoop, ReducedLoop, Prompt-Refine) within the framework.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is moderate. The main advantage is the *training-free* nature of the approach. Fine-tuning LLMs can be computationally expensive, so a training-free method that can generate high-quality data is valuable. The demonstration that TAGAL achieves competitive performance with training-based methods is promising. However, the practical impact hinges on the generalizability and scalability of the approach to more complex and diverse tabular datasets.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Training-free:** The approach avoids the computational cost of LLM fine-tuning.
    *   **Competitive Performance:**  TAGAL shows promising results, achieving performance comparable to training-based methods and outperforming other training-free approaches.
    *   **Modular Design:** The three methods (SynthLoop, ReducedLoop, Prompt-Refine) offer different trade-offs between computation time and data quality.
    *   **Extensibility:** The prompts can be modified to incorporate expert knowledge or target specific data characteristics.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependence:** The approach is heavily reliant on the quality and biases of the underlying LLMs.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The performance of the method is sensitive to the prompt design, meta-parameters, and choice of LLMs.
    *   **Computational cost (at least for the base model):** Base method (SynthLoop) can take many iterations. The speed ups of other methods might not be high enough.
    *   **Data Contamination:** While the paper acknowledges the issue of data contamination, it does not provide a comprehensive analysis of the extent to which it affects the results, and does not fully alleviate such contamination issues.
    *   **Limited theoretical foundation:** There are is not an indepth explanation of what factors are important.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research on synthetic tabular data generation by highlighting the benefits of agentic workflows and training-free approaches. It also encourages further investigation into the use of external knowledge and feedback mechanisms for improving data quality.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a promising training-free approach for generating synthetic tabular data using agentic LLMs. However, the novelty of the approach is incremental, and the practical impact remains to be seen. The paper acknowledges some limitations.

**Score: 6**

*   **Rationale:** The score of 6 reflects that the paper has *some* novelty and *moderate* significance. The training-free aspect and competitive performance are notable strengths. However, the dependence on LLMs, parameter sensitivity, issues with data contamination, and lack of a deeper theoretical explanation, limit the overall impact and justify a score below a 7.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Real Time FPGA Based Transformers & VLMs for Vision Tasks: SOTA Designs and Optimizations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04162v1)
- **Authors**: Safa Mohammed Sali, Mahmoud Meribout, Ashiyana Abdul Majeed
- **Abstract**: Transformers and vision-language models (VLMs) have emerged as dominant architectures in computer vision and multimodal AI, offering state-of-the-art performance in tasks such as image classification, object detection, visual question answering, and caption generation. However, their high computational complexity, large memory footprints, and irregular data access patterns present significant challenges for deployment in latency- and power-constrained environments. Field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) provide an attractive hardware platform for such workloads due to their reconfigurability, fine-grained parallelism, and potential for energy-efficient acceleration. This paper presents a comprehensive review of design trade-offs, optimization strategies, and implementation challenges for FPGA-based inference of transformers and VLMs. We examine critical factors such as device-class selection, memory subsystem constraints, dataflow orchestration, quantization strategies, sparsity exploitation, and toolchain choices, alongside modality-specific issues unique to VLMs, including heterogeneous compute balancing and cross-attention memory management. Additionally, we discuss emerging trends in hardware-algorithm co-design, highlighting innovations in attention mechanisms, compression, and modular overlays to improve efficiency and adaptability. Practical issues such as runtime flexibility, verification overhead, and the absence of standardized FPGA multimodal benchmarks are also considered. Finally, we outline future directions toward scalable, portable, and reconfigurable FPGA solutions that adapt to evolving model architectures while sustaining high utilization and predictable performance. This synthesis offers both a technical foundation and a forward-looking perspective to help bridge the gap between advanced multimodal AI models and efficient FPGA deployment.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive review of FPGA-based acceleration strategies for Transformer and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), focusing on architectural innovations, optimization techniques, and emerging software tools. It delves into design trade-offs, optimization strategies like quantization and sparsity exploitation, and implementation challenges specific to FPGA-based inference of Transformers and VLMs. It examines factors such as device selection, memory constraints, dataflow orchestration, and modality-specific issues in VLMs. The paper also discusses emerging trends like hardware-algorithm co-design, highlights innovations in attention mechanisms, and addresses practical considerations such as runtime flexibility and verification complexity. Finally, it identifies future research directions focused on scalable, portable, and reconfigurable FPGA solutions that adapt to evolving model architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems primarily from its focus on the intersection of FPGAs and VLMs. While surveys exist on FPGA acceleration for Transformers and LLMs individually, a consolidated review specifically addressing VLMs on FPGAs, encompassing the latest advancements (like the Versal device family and sparsity optimization), fills a noticeable gap. The paper also provides a timely snapshot given the recent surge in VLM research. The claim of focusing on latest (2025) advances should be critically viewed because it is unlikely that the all the claimed advancement will be available in the start of the year 2025.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to guide researchers and engineers in bridging the gap between cutting-edge multimodal AI models and efficient, real-world FPGA deployment. The comprehensive review of optimization techniques, hardware architectures, and software tools provides a valuable resource for those working in this rapidly evolving field. The identification of future research directions further contributes to its potential impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensiveness:** The paper covers a wide range of topics relevant to FPGA-based VLM acceleration, including architectural considerations, optimization techniques, software tools, and application-specific challenges.
    *   **Timeliness:** The review focuses on recent advancements in the field, providing an up-to-date perspective on the current state of the art.
    *   **Practical Guidance:** The paper offers practical insights into design trade-offs, implementation challenges, and optimization strategies, making it a valuable resource for practitioners.
    *   **Well-Organized:** The paper is structured logically, making it easy to navigate and understand the key concepts.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Lack of Quantitative Comparison:** While the paper discusses various optimization techniques, it often lacks quantitative comparisons of their effectiveness in the context of FPGA-based VLM acceleration.  More concrete examples and performance metrics would strengthen the analysis. There is a lack of fair comparisons because of the use of different datasets.
    *   **Limited Experimental Validation:** The paper primarily focuses on reviewing existing research. The absence of original experimental results or case studies demonstrating the effectiveness of specific FPGA-based VLM implementations limits its practical impact. The claims about future trends should be balanced with current limitations and the degree of speculation.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While comprehensive, some sections could benefit from a deeper dive into specific optimization techniques or hardware architectures. For instance, a more detailed analysis of the trade-offs between different memory architectures or dataflow strategies would be valuable. The survey does not go to sufficient depth of the different techniques and mostly touches on the surface of each techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a centralized resource for researchers and engineers working on FPGA-based VLM acceleration. It can help guide future research efforts by identifying promising areas for innovation and addressing the key challenges in the field.

**Overall:**

The paper is a well-structured and timely review that makes a useful contribution by consolidating the dispersed literature. It offers a valuable introduction and overview, but falls short of a deeper analytical or experimental contribution.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's good quality as a comprehensive and up-to-date review, but acknowledges the limitations in quantitative analysis, experimental validation, and depth of analysis. While significant, the paper's impact is somewhat limited by its lack of original research and concrete performance evaluations. The core strength is to bring together available recent results to guide researchers and engineers in bridging the gap between the fast developed VLMs and real world FPGA deployment.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Privacy Risks in Time Series Forecasting: User- and Record-Level Membership Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04169v1)
- **Authors**: Nicolas Johansson, Tobias Olsson, Daniel Nilsson, Johan Östman, Fazeleh Hoseini
- **Abstract**: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) aim to determine whether specific data were used to train a model. While extensively studied on classification models, their impact on time series forecasting remains largely unexplored. We address this gap by introducing two new attacks: (i) an adaptation of multivariate LiRA, a state-of-the-art MIA originally developed for classification models, to the time-series forecasting setting, and (ii) a novel end-to-end learning approach called Deep Time Series (DTS) attack. We benchmark these methods against adapted versions of other leading attacks from the classification setting. We evaluate all attacks in realistic settings on the TUH-EEG and ELD datasets, targeting two strong forecasting architectures, LSTM and the state-of-the-art N-HiTS, under both record- and user-level threat models. Our results show that forecasting models are vulnerable, with user-level attacks often achieving perfect detection. The proposed methods achieve the strongest performance in several settings, establishing new baselines for privacy risk assessment in time series forecasting. Furthermore, vulnerability increases with longer prediction horizons and smaller training populations, echoing trends observed in large language models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates membership inference attacks (MIAs) against time series forecasting models. It addresses the existing gap in research by introducing two new attacks: a multivariate adaptation of LiRA (Likelihood Ratio Attack) for time series and a novel end-to-end learning approach called Deep Time Series (DTS) attack. These attacks are benchmarked against adapted versions of existing MIA methods originally designed for classification tasks. The evaluation is performed on the TUH-EEG and ELD datasets, targeting LSTM and N-HiTS forecasting architectures, under both record-level and user-level threat models. The results demonstrate the vulnerability of forecasting models to MIAs, with user-level attacks achieving particularly high detection rates. The proposed methods often outperform existing techniques, establishing new baselines for privacy risk assessment in time series forecasting. The paper also highlights that vulnerability increases with longer prediction horizons and smaller training populations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces two new attacks specifically designed for time series forecasting: Multivariate LiRA adaptation and the Deep Time Series (DTS) attack. While LiRA itself is not new, its adaptation and application to the time series domain, along with the development of DTS (a learning-based approach bypassing feature engineering) brings novel insights. The systematic evaluation of various MIAs in the time-series forecasting context, including adaptations of classification-based attacks, is a significant contribution. Previously, research was limited.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addresses a Gap:** It fills a critical gap in the MIA literature by focusing on time series forecasting models, a domain with growing importance and sensitive applications.
    *   **Realistic Threat Models:** The evaluation uses real-world datasets and considers both record-level and user-level threat models, making the findings more relevant to practical scenarios.
    *   **Establishes Baselines:** The paper establishes new baselines for evaluating the privacy risks of time series forecasting models, paving the way for future research in this area.
    *   **Identifies Key Factors:**  The analysis of how prediction horizon and training population size affect vulnerability provides valuable insights for designing more privacy-preserving forecasting models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation comparing multiple attacks, target models, and datasets.
    *   **Strong Results:** The proposed DTS attack often achieves state-of-the-art performance, especially in the record-level setting, demonstrating its effectiveness. The high detection rates achieved by user-level attacks highlight the severity of privacy risks in this domain.
    *   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written and clearly structured, making it easy to follow the methodology and understand the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on two specific datasets (TUH-EEG and ELD) and two forecasting architectures (LSTM and N-HiTS). While these are representative, further evaluation on a wider range of datasets and models would increase the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Independence Assumption:** The user-level attack assumes independence of record-level predictions, which might not always hold in practice. Exploring more sophisticated aggregation methods could improve the attack's performance.  While acknowledged, it's a limitation.
    *   **Limited Number of Users:** The comparatively small number of users in the datasets could introduce instability in some results (particularly user-level attacks) and affects the estimates of covariance matrices in multi-signal LiRA.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to significantly influence research in privacy-preserving time series forecasting. It provides practical methods for assessing privacy risks and highlights the importance of considering user-level vulnerabilities. The findings could inform the development of new defense mechanisms against MIAs and the design of more privacy-conscious forecasting models. The paper will serve as a reference point for future studies in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers significant contributions to an under-explored area by adapting MIA techniques and proposing a novel attack specific to time series forecasting. It presents a thorough evaluation, establishes new baselines, and identifies crucial factors influencing vulnerability. While having some limitations, its impact on the field is substantial.
Score: 8.0

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### MAGneT: Coordinated Multi-Agent Generation of Synthetic Multi-Turn Mental Health Counseling Sessions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04183v1)
- **Authors**: Aishik Mandal, Tanmoy Chakraborty, Iryna Gurevych
- **Abstract**: The growing demand for scalable psychological counseling highlights the need for fine-tuning open-source Large Language Models (LLMs) with high-quality, privacy-compliant data, yet such data remains scarce. Here we introduce MAGneT, a novel multi-agent framework for synthetic psychological counseling session generation that decomposes counselor response generation into coordinated sub-tasks handled by specialized LLM agents, each modeling a key psychological technique. Unlike prior single-agent approaches, MAGneT better captures the structure and nuance of real counseling. In addition, we address inconsistencies in prior evaluation protocols by proposing a unified evaluation framework integrating diverse automatic and expert metrics. Furthermore, we expand the expert evaluations from four aspects of counseling in previous works to nine aspects, enabling a more thorough and robust assessment of data quality. Empirical results show that MAGneT significantly outperforms existing methods in quality, diversity, and therapeutic alignment of the generated counseling sessions, improving general counseling skills by 3.2% and CBT-specific skills by 4.3% on average on cognitive therapy rating scale (CTRS). Crucially, experts prefer MAGneT-generated sessions in 77.2% of cases on average across all aspects. Moreover, fine-tuning an open-source model on MAGneT-generated sessions shows better performance, with improvements of 6.3% on general counseling skills and 7.3% on CBT-specific skills on average on CTRS over those fine-tuned with sessions generated by baseline methods. We also make our code and data public.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary, critical evaluation, and novelty score for the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAGneT, a multi-agent framework for generating synthetic multi-turn mental health counseling sessions. It addresses the limitations of previous single-agent approaches by decomposing counselor response generation into coordinated sub-tasks handled by specialized LLM agents, each modeling a specific psychological technique (reflection, questioning, solution provision, normalization, and psycho-education). A technique selector and CBT planning agent coordinate the agent's activities. The paper also proposes a unified evaluation framework integrating diverse automatic and expert metrics, expanding expert evaluation to nine aspects of counseling for more robust assessment.  Empirical results show that MAGneT outperforms existing methods in quality, diversity, and therapeutic alignment, with expert preferences favoring MAGneT-generated sessions in a substantial proportion of cases. Fine-tuning an open-source model on MAGneT-generated sessions further improves performance, highlighting its utility in training counseling agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Architecture:** The multi-agent approach is a significant departure from existing single-agent or simple role-playing methods, offering a more structured and psychologically grounded way to simulate counseling sessions.  The design, grounded in therapeutic techniques and CBT principles, addresses a crucial gap in current synthetic data generation.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The unified evaluation framework, incorporating both automatic metrics and an expanded expert evaluation, provides a more rigorous and reliable assessment of the generated data.  This tackles a significant problem in the field of inconsistent evaluation methods, making comparison of different methods difficult.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper demonstrates the superiority of MAGneT through extensive experiments, including comparisons with strong baselines, ablation studies, fine-tuning experiments, and expert evaluation.
    *   **Practical Utility:** The demonstration of improved performance after fine-tuning an open-source model on MAGneT-generated data highlights its practical value in training real-world counseling agents.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance hinges on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs.  Potential biases or limitations within the LLMs could affect the quality and diversity of the generated sessions.
    *   **Complexity:**  The multi-agent architecture adds complexity to the system. The computational cost of running the multiple agents might be higher than simpler single-agent approaches.
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the paper demonstrates strong performance within the CBT framework, it's unclear how well MAGneT would generalize to other therapeutic approaches.
    *   **Negative Attitude Client Challenges:**  The framework still shows similar limitations, such as lower performance with clients with negative attitudes, suggesting a need for further refinement to handle such cases.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Advancing Synthetic Data Generation:** MAGneT significantly advances the state-of-the-art in synthetic counseling data generation, providing a valuable resource for training and developing mental health support systems.
    *   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** By generating high-quality, privacy-compliant data, MAGneT helps to overcome the challenge of limited access to real counseling data, enabling the development of more accessible and scalable mental health solutions.
    *   **Standardizing Evaluation:** The unified evaluation framework contributes to the standardization of data generation and model assessment, fostering more reliable comparison of different approaches and promoting further research in the field.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated approach to synthetic counseling data generation. The strengths of the paper (novel architecture, comprehensive evaluation, empirical validation, and practical utility) outweigh the weaknesses (reliance on LLMs, complexity, and limited generalizability). The unified evaluation and thorough empirical testing make this a significant contribution to the field.
The innovative approach and thorough analysis warrant a high score, reflecting its positive impact on the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### KubeGuard: LLM-Assisted Kubernetes Hardening via Configuration Files and Runtime Logs Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04191v1)
- **Authors**: Omri Sgan Cohen, Ehud Malul, Yair Meidan, Dudu Mimran, Yuval Elovici, Asaf Shabtai
- **Abstract**: The widespread adoption of Kubernetes (K8s) for orchestrating cloud-native applications has introduced significant security challenges, such as misconfigured resources and overly permissive configurations. Failing to address these issues can result in unauthorized access, privilege escalation, and lateral movement within clusters. Most existing K8s security solutions focus on detecting misconfigurations, typically through static analysis or anomaly detection. In contrast, this paper presents KubeGuard, a novel runtime log-driven recommender framework aimed at mitigating risks by addressing overly permissive configurations. KubeGuard is designed to harden K8s environments through two complementary tasks: Resource Creation and Resource Refinement. It leverages large language models (LLMs) to analyze manifests and runtime logs reflecting actual system behavior, using modular prompt-chaining workflows. This approach enables KubeGuard to create least-privilege configurations for new resources and refine existing manifests to reduce the attack surface. KubeGuard's output manifests are presented as recommendations that users (e.g., developers and operators) can review and adopt to enhance cluster security. Our evaluation demonstrates that KubeGuard effectively generates and refines K8s manifests for Roles, NetworkPolicies, and Deployments, leveraging both proprietary and open-source LLMs. The high precision, recall, and F1-scores affirm KubeGuard's practicality as a framework that translates runtime observability into actionable, least-privilege configuration guidance.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KubeGuard, a novel framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to enhance Kubernetes (K8s) security by addressing overly permissive configurations. KubeGuard dynamically hardens K8s environments through two main tasks: Resource Creation (generating secure manifests for new resources) and Resource Refinement (hardening existing manifests by removing excessive permissions). The framework leverages multi-source runtime logs (audit, network, provenance) and modular prompt-chaining workflows to analyze system behavior and generate least-privilege configuration recommendations. The paper evaluates KubeGuard using both proprietary and open-source LLMs, demonstrating its effectiveness in generating and refining manifests for Roles, NetworkPolicies, and Deployments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of runtime log analysis with LLM-based manifest generation and refinement, taking a prompt-chaining approach. While existing tools focus mostly on static analysis or anomaly detection, KubeGuard dynamically adapts security configurations based on observed application behavior. The explicit use of provenance data to inform configuration hardening adds another layer of innovation, connecting runtime events to configuration changes. This constitutes a distinct improvement over existing misconfiguration detection approaches that predominantly use static analysis. The context-aware configuration optimization in KubeGuard, analyzing multi-source logs, and the agnostic-model prompt chaining are a distinctive step towards a more comprehensive and dynamic approach to hardening resources, including the RBAC settings, which are also an important advance.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in Kubernetes security: misconfigurations leading to unauthorized access and lateral movement. By offering a proactive, log-driven hardening framework, KubeGuard can significantly improve the security posture of K8s clusters. The framework's ability to leverage both proprietary (GPT-40) and open-source (Llama-3.1-8B) LLMs makes it accessible to organizations with varying resource and privacy constraints. The comprehensive evaluation demonstrates KubeGuard's effectiveness in multiple tasks (Role Creation/Refinement, NetworkPolicy Creation/Refinement, Deployment Refinement) and with different LLMs, adding to its significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive approach:** KubeGuard addresses both the creation and refinement of K8s resources, providing a complete solution for hardening K8s security.
    *   **Runtime log-driven:** The framework leverages runtime logs (audit, network, provenance) to dynamically adapt security configurations based on observed application behavior.
    *   **LLM-based reasoning:** KubeGuard uses LLMs to analyze manifests and runtime logs, enabling context-aware configuration generation and refinement.
    *   **Model-agnostic architecture:** The framework supports both proprietary and open-source LLMs, providing flexibility across different organizational constraints.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of KubeGuard using two microservice-based applications and multiple prompting methods, model categories, and tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited resource coverage:** KubeGuard currently targets only Roles, NetworkPolicies, and Deployments. Expanding support to other K8s resources (e.g., ConfigMaps, PersistentVolumes) would make the framework more comprehensive.
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The framework's performance is dependent on the LLM's capabilities and prompt engineering. Poorly designed prompts or limitations of the LLM may affect the quality of the generated manifests.
    *   **Human-in-the-loop enforcement:** KubeGuard generates recommendations but does not automatically enforce them, requiring human review and approval. This may limit adoption in environments where automated remediation is desired. This decision, however, does enhance operational security and reduces the risk of breaking functionality.
    *   Some of the performance variations based on the model used raise questions of practicality with smaller LLMs, indicating the need to improve their capability.
*   **Potential Influence:** KubeGuard has the potential to significantly influence the field of K8s security by shifting the focus from static analysis and anomaly detection to dynamic, log-driven hardening. The framework's modular design and support for different LLMs make it adaptable to various environments and organizational needs. The insights gained from this research can also inform the development of more advanced security solutions for other cloud-native platforms.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** KubeGuard presents a significant advancement in K8s security by combining runtime log analysis with LLM-based manifest generation and refinement. The framework's comprehensive approach, model-agnostic architecture, and thorough evaluation make it a promising solution for hardening K8s environments. While it has some limitations, KubeGuard's innovative approach and potential influence on the field justify a score of 8. The primary reasons for not assigning a higher score are the limited resource coverage and the human-in-the-loop enforcement, which may limit its immediate adoption in certain environments.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Are LLM Agents the New RPA? A Comparative Study with RPA Across Enterprise Workflows
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04198v1)
- **Authors**: Petr Průcha, Michaela Matoušková, Jan Strnad
- **Abstract**: The emergence of large language models (LLMs) has introduced a new paradigm in automation: LLM agents or Agentic Automation with Computer Use (AACU). Unlike traditional Robotic Process Automation (RPA), which relies on rule-based workflows and scripting, AACU enables intelligent agents to perform tasks through natural language instructions and autonomous interaction with user interfaces. This study investigates whether AACU can serve as a viable alternative to RPA in enterprise workflow automation. We conducted controlled experiments across three standard RPA challenges data entry, monitoring, and document extraction comparing RPA (via UiPath) and AACU (via Anthropic's Computer Use Agent) in terms of speed, reliability, and development effort. Results indicate that RPA outperforms AACU in execution speed and reliability, particularly in repetitive, stable environments. However, AACU significantly reduces development time and adapts more flexibly to dynamic interfaces. While current AACU implementations are not yet production-ready, their promise in rapid prototyping and lightweight automation is evident. Future research should explore multi-agent orchestration, hybrid RPA-AACU architectures, and more robust evaluation across industries and platforms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study between LLM-based agents (specifically Anthropic's Computer Use Agent) and traditional Robotic Process Automation (RPA) (UiPath) across three standard RPA challenges: data entry, monitoring, and document extraction. The study evaluates both technologies based on speed, reliability, and development effort. The results show that RPA outperforms AACU in speed and reliability, especially in stable environments. However, AACU significantly reduces development time and demonstrates greater adaptability to dynamic interfaces.  The authors conclude that while AACU is not yet production-ready, its potential for rapid prototyping and lightweight automation is evident and warrants further research into multi-agent orchestration, hybrid architectures, and broader industrial evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical Comparison:** The paper directly addresses a timely and relevant question: Can LLM agents replace RPA? It backs up claims with empirical data obtained from controlled experiments.  This is a significant strength, moving beyond purely theoretical discussions.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The choice of RPA challenges as test cases is appropriate and allows for a structured comparison.
    * **Transparency:**  The authors detail their experimental setup, including software versions, agent configurations, and the prompts used.  They also acknowledge limitations and provide access to their data and code on GitHub.
    * **Nuanced Analysis:**  The paper avoids overstating the potential of AACU. The authors acknowledge the current limitations while highlighting its strengths in development speed and adaptability. They are fair to the state of both technologies.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study is restricted to a small set of three RPA challenges.  Extrapolating these results to a broader range of enterprise workflows is questionable. Furthermore, using a single agentic automation tool (Anthropic's Computer Use Agent) and only one implementation of RPA (UiPath) restricts the generalizability of the findings. It’s entirely possible that other tools show different results.
    * **Small Sample Size:** While the paper performs 10 runs for each experiment (except the one where LLM agent failed due to technical issues), the overall sample size is still relatively small. This impacts the statistical power of the conclusions, particularly regarding the reliability analysis.
    * **Development Time Measurement:** The measurement of development time is subjective and relies on a single developer's experience. The claim that it is an *empirical* value is misleading, as it only measures the experience of a single developer. While the paper acknowledges this, it's a significant limitation.
    * **Maturity of AACU:** The AACU technology is in its early stages, and its limitations are to be expected. Directly comparing a nascent technology with a well-established one like RPA is inherently biased. However, the authors are up front about this.
    * **Statistical Analysis:** The choice of the Fisher's Exact Test is appropriate for the reliability analysis (H2) given the small sample sizes. Welch's t-test is also correct. However, drawing strong conclusions from such limited data is still problematic, and it could be stated more carefully, even if the selection of the analysis method is correct.
    * **Accessibility to Challenges:** In a true research effort, if the research is supposed to be repeatable, ensuring that links to all assets is key, or that all assets are provided. Some of the web links in the paper were dead, which hinders repeatability of the experiments.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes to the field by providing an early empirical evaluation of LLM agents as a potential alternative to RPA. While other research explores the *potential* of AI agents in automation, this study directly compares them with established RPA technologies across realistic tasks. The findings offer valuable insights for practitioners and researchers considering the adoption of AACU.  The framework provided by the authors is valuable, and the insights gained can be built on for follow-up research and comparison with other LLM Agents and RPA technologies. The study raises important questions about the trade-offs between speed, reliability, and development effort when choosing automation technologies. It identifies key areas for future research, such as hybrid RPA-AACU architectures.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper has merit due to its empirical approach and insightful analysis of AACU and RPA. It is one of the earlier studies in this emerging area. However, the limitations in scope, sample size, and the developmental stage of AACU warrant a lower score. The limitations on repeatability also impact the score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### Explicit and Implicit Data Augmentation for Social Event Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04202v1)
- **Authors**: Congbo Ma, Yuxia Wang, Jia Wu, Jian Yang, Jing Du, Zitai Qiu, Qing Li, Hu Wang, Preslav Nakov
- **Abstract**: Social event detection involves identifying and categorizing important events from social media, which relies on labeled data, but annotation is costly and labor-intensive. To address this problem, we propose Augmentation framework for Social Event Detection (SED-Aug), a plug-and-play dual augmentation framework, which combines explicit text-based and implicit feature-space augmentation to enhance data diversity and model robustness. The explicit augmentation utilizes large language models to enhance textual information through five diverse generation strategies. For implicit augmentation, we design five novel perturbation techniques that operate in the feature space on structural fused embeddings. These perturbations are crafted to keep the semantic and relational properties of the embeddings and make them more diverse. Specifically, SED-Aug outperforms the best baseline model by approximately 17.67% on the Twitter2012 dataset and by about 15.57% on the Twitter2018 dataset in terms of the average F1 score. The code is available at GitHub: https://github.com/congboma/SED-Aug.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SED-Aug, a novel data augmentation framework for Social Event Detection (SED). SED-Aug is a plug-and-play dual augmentation approach that combines explicit text-based augmentation using Large Language Models (LLMs) with implicit feature-space augmentation. The explicit augmentation involves five different LLM-based strategies (paraphrasing, context addition, style transfer, entity preservation, and extraction-rewrite). The implicit augmentation applies five novel perturbation techniques in the feature space of structural-fused embeddings (Gaussian Perturbation, Proportional Gaussian Perturbation, In-Distribution Gaussian Perturbation, Clipped Gaussian Perturbation, and Frequency-Domain Perturbation). The authors demonstrate that SED-Aug outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on Twitter2012 and Twitter2018 datasets in terms of F1 score.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of explicit (LLM-based) and implicit (feature-space perturbation) data augmentation is a solid contribution, especially for SED. Augmenting graph-based data, which is often overlooked, makes this approach novel.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The paper details a wide variety of augmentation techniques both in the textual domain and in the feature space. The systematic evaluation of various combinations adds to the thoroughness of the research.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results show significant improvements over strong baseline models on multiple datasets. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
*   **Addressing a real-world problem:** The paper tackles a fundamental challenge in SED – the scarcity of labeled data – with a practical and effective solution.
*   **Well-Structured and Well-Explained:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand the proposed methods and experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM Hallucination**: Adding context using LLMs can lead to the generation of false or misleading information.
*   **Dataset limitations**: While the improvements are notable, the performance is mostly tested on two older Twitter datasets, which may not reflect the characteristics of current social media environments. Testing on more diverse and contemporary datasets is needed to assess the generalizability of the framework.
*   **Lack of theoretical justification**: The choice of specific feature-space perturbation techniques is somewhat ad-hoc. A stronger theoretical justification for why these particular perturbations are expected to be effective would strengthen the paper.
*   **Complexity**: The framework is quite complex, involving multiple components and hyperparameters. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the sensitivity of the results to different hyperparameter settings.
*   **Impact of noise**: Adding too much noise in the implicit space can reduce the effectiveness of the perturbation techniques.

**Significance:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of SED by offering a practical and effective data augmentation framework. The combination of explicit and implicit augmentation techniques has the potential to improve the performance of SED models in low-resource scenarios and enhance model robustness. The paper's findings could influence future research on SED and other related tasks involving graph data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty lies in the dual augmentation approach and the specific techniques used for feature-space perturbation on structural data. The empirical results are convincing, showing significant improvements over strong baselines. However, the reliance on relatively old datasets and the lack of stronger theoretical grounding for the feature-space augmentation limit its overall impact. Given these considerations, the paper represents a solid but not groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 7/10

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking the long-range dependency in Mamba/SSM and transformer models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04226v1)
- **Authors**: Cong Ma, Kayvan Najarian
- **Abstract**: Long-range dependency is one of the most desired properties of recent sequence models such as state-space models (particularly Mamba) and transformer models. New model architectures are being actively developed and benchmarked for prediction tasks requiring long-range dependency. However, the capability of modeling long-range dependencies of these models has not been investigated from a theoretical perspective, which hinders a systematic improvement on this aspect. In this work, we mathematically define long-range dependency using the derivative of hidden states with respect to past inputs and compare the capability of SSM and transformer models of modeling long-range dependency based on this definition. We showed that the long-range dependency of SSM decays exponentially with the sequence length, which aligns with the exponential decay of memory function in RNN. But the attention mechanism used in transformers is more flexible and is not constrained to exponential decay, which could in theory perform better at modeling long-range dependency with sufficient training data, computing resources, and proper training. To combine the flexibility of long-range dependency of attention mechanism and computation efficiency of SSM, we propose a new formulation for hidden state update in SSM and prove its stability under a standard Gaussian distribution of the input data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the long-range dependency (LRD) capabilities of Mamba/SSM and Transformer models, both prominent architectures in sequence modeling. It mathematically defines LRD using the derivative of hidden states with respect to past inputs and compares the two models based on this definition. The analysis reveals that SSM's LRD decays exponentially, aligning with RNN's memory function behavior, while the attention mechanism in Transformers, theoretically, has more flexibility and isn't constrained to exponential decay, but requires more data. The paper then proposes a novel hidden state update formulation for SSM, aiming to combine Transformer-like LRD flexibility with SSM's computational efficiency, proving its stability under Gaussian input.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The theoretical comparison of LRD capabilities between SSM and Transformers is a valuable contribution. While practitioners have observed performance differences, a formal mathematical definition and analysis provides a deeper understanding. The proposed new SSM formulation with an "interaction" term is also novel, attempting to bridge the gap between the two architectures.

*   **Significance:** Understanding the strengths and limitations of different architectures in modeling LRD is crucial for developing more effective sequence models. The paper identifies a key constraint in SSMs (exponential decay) and proposes a modification. This could potentially lead to new architectures that better capture LRD, improving performance on tasks where long-range dependencies are essential. The paper's theoretical focus is a welcome departure from purely empirical evaluations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Provides a clear mathematical definition of LRD.
    *   Offers a comparative theoretical analysis of SSM/Mamba and Transformers concerning LRD.
    *   Proposes a novel SSM formulation to address LRD limitations.
    *   Includes a stability analysis of the proposed formulation.
    *   The empirical component with simulation adds validation.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis relies on some simplifying assumptions (e.g., scalar inputs, specific matrix diagonalizations) which may limit its generalizability.
    *   The stability analysis is somewhat limited, focusing on a specific case and probabilistic tail bounds. A more comprehensive stability analysis would be beneficial.
    *   While the simulations demonstrate the non-exponential decay, they don't show definitive performance gains in practical LRD tasks.
    *   The proof of theorem 4 seems somewhat lacking in mathematical rigor (specifically, the chain of Jensen's inequalities may require further elaboration to be fully justified).

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper provides a useful theoretical analysis of LRD in two important sequence modeling architectures. While the proposed formulation has promising potential, it requires further empirical validation and a more robust stability analysis. The work is theoretically sound but needs further validation to demonstrate its impact. Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 6 is appropriate.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### How many patients could we save with LLM priors?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04250v1)
- **Authors**: Shota Arai, David Selby, Andrew Vargo, Sebastian Vollmer
- **Abstract**: Imagine a world where clinical trials need far fewer patients to achieve the same statistical power, thanks to the knowledge encoded in large language models (LLMs). We present a novel framework for hierarchical Bayesian modeling of adverse events in multi-center clinical trials, leveraging LLM-informed prior distributions. Unlike data augmentation approaches that generate synthetic data points, our methodology directly obtains parametric priors from the model. Our approach systematically elicits informative priors for hyperparameters in hierarchical Bayesian models using a pre-trained LLM, enabling the incorporation of external clinical expertise directly into Bayesian safety modeling. Through comprehensive temperature sensitivity analysis and rigorous cross-validation on real-world clinical trial data, we demonstrate that LLM-derived priors consistently improve predictive performance compared to traditional meta-analytical approaches. This methodology paves the way for more efficient and expert-informed clinical trial design, enabling substantial reductions in the number of patients required to achieve robust safety assessment and with the potential to transform drug safety monitoring and regulatory decision making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate informative prior distributions for hierarchical Bayesian modeling of adverse events (AEs) in multi-center clinical trials.  Instead of data augmentation, the paper directly elicits parametric priors from LLMs.  The authors compare LLM-derived priors (using Llama 3.3 and MedGemma) with traditional meta-analytical priors on a real-world non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) clinical trial dataset. They assess performance through cross-validation and sample efficiency analysis.  The results suggest that LLM-informed priors can improve predictive performance and reduce the number of patients required in clinical trials to achieve robust safety assessments. The paper concludes that this approach can lead to more efficient and expert-informed trial designs. The best results are obtained with Llama 3.3 using a blind prompt at a temperature of 1.0.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to elicit priors for hierarchical Bayesian models in clinical trials has some novelty, but it builds directly on existing research in LLM-based prior elicitation, especially the prior work of Selby et al. (2025). The application to the specific context of adverse event modeling in multi-center clinical trials and the focus on sample size reduction is a worthwhile contribution. The authors are careful to distinguish their approach from data augmentation techniques, emphasizing the direct improvement of the statistical model itself.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work lies in the ability to reduce the number of patients needed in clinical trials while maintaining statistical power. If validated across different disease areas and datasets, this could lead to significant cost savings and reduced patient burden. The work addresses a practical problem in clinical trial design: how to incorporate domain expertise into statistical models in a principled way. However, the magnitude of the improvement (LPD values) isn't dramatically different from the meta-analytical baseline, tempering enthusiasm somewhat. The study's focus on a single disease (NSCLC) limits its generalizability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper uses a real-world clinical trial dataset for evaluation, making the results more relevant and practical.
    *   The experimental design is well-structured, including cross-validation and sample efficiency analysis.
    *   The temperature sensitivity analysis is a good practice for ensuring the robustness of the LLM-derived priors.
    *   The comparison to a well-established meta-analytical prior (Barmaz and Ménard, 2021) provides a strong baseline.
    *   The authors are careful to control the prompts, ensuring consistent formatting and minimizing confounding factors.
    *   The code will be shared, enhancing reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The study focuses on a single dataset (NSCLC). Validation on other diseases and types of clinical endpoints is needed to demonstrate generalizability.
    *   Only two LLMs (Llama 3.3 and MedGemma) are evaluated. Expanding the evaluation to other models could provide a more comprehensive picture.
    *   The magnitude of the performance improvement (while statistically significant) is not overwhelmingly large. The clinical significance of these improvements may require further investigation.
    *   Disease-informed prompts do not consistently outperform blind prompts, suggesting that sophisticated prompt engineering may not always be necessary. This may be a strength (simpler is better), but the authors don't fully explore the implications.
    *   The paper only focuses on rate parameters from exponential priors, which is a simplified representation of domain knowledge. Future work can investigate estimating other types of priors or hyperparameters.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research into LLM-based prior elicitation for clinical trials and other statistical modeling applications. It could potentially influence clinical trial design practices if the results are replicated and validated in broader contexts. The demonstration of sample efficiency gains is particularly compelling.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the incremental novelty, limited generalizability (single dataset), and modest performance improvements, a score of 6 seems appropriate. The work is technically sound, well-written, and addresses a relevant problem, but it lacks the breakthrough potential required for a higher score. The fact that prior work already exists using LLMs for prior elicitation diminishes the degree of innovation. While the sample efficiency gain is promising, more work is needed to assess clinical significance across broader contexts.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: stat.ME
- **Score**: 6/10

### RL's Razor: Why Online Reinforcement Learning Forgets Less
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04259v1)
- **Authors**: Idan Shenfeld, Jyothish Pari, Pulkit Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Comparison of fine-tuning models with reinforcement learning (RL) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) reveals that, despite similar performance at a new task, RL preserves prior knowledge and capabilities significantly better. We find that the degree of forgetting is determined by the distributional shift, measured as the KL-divergence between the fine-tuned and base policy evaluated on the new task. Our analysis reveals that on-policy RL is implicitly biased towards KL-minimal solutions among the many that solve the new task, whereas SFT can converge to distributions arbitrarily far from the base model. We validate these findings through experiments with large language models and robotic foundation models and further provide theoretical justification for why on-policy RL updates lead to a smaller KL change. We term this principle $\textit{RL's Razor}$: among all ways to solve a new task, RL prefers those closest in KL to the original model.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "RL's Razor: Why Online Reinforcement Learning Forgets Less" investigates why Reinforcement Learning (RL) fine-tuning of foundation models leads to less catastrophic forgetting compared to Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), even when both methods achieve similar performance on the new task.  The authors' core finding is that on-policy RL implicitly biases learning toward solutions that minimize the KL divergence between the fine-tuned policy and the original, pre-trained policy. They term this principle "RL's Razor."  The paper presents empirical evidence from large language models (LLMs) and robotic foundation models, along with theoretical justification, to support this KL-minimization bias. They further demonstrate that minimizing KL divergence, even in an SFT setting, leads to reduced forgetting.  The paper proposes that explicitly minimizing KL divergence during post-training is a key principle for achieving continual adaptation without catastrophic forgetting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of connecting RL's superior resistance to catastrophic forgetting to its implicit KL-minimization bias is novel and insightful. While previous work has identified KL regularization as a useful heuristic, this paper elevates it to a fundamental principle governing forgetting. The empirical "forgetting law" that quantifies the relationship between KL divergence on the new task and the degree of forgetting is a valuable contribution. The theoretical justification, while simplified, provides a plausible explanation for this bias.
* **Significance:** Catastrophic forgetting is a major hurdle in the development of long-lived AI agents and continually adapting foundation models. This paper provides a valuable perspective and a tangible direction for future research. By identifying KL divergence as a key determinant of forgetting, the authors offer a practical guideline for developing improved post-training methods. The principle of "RL's Razor" highlights the importance of maintaining proximity to the base model's distribution during adaptation.
* **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical support: The paper demonstrates the KL-minimization bias across diverse domains (LLMs, robotics) and model architectures, strengthening the validity of their claim.
    *   Theoretical justification: The theoretical analysis, though simplified, provides a convincing argument for why on-policy methods should naturally minimize KL divergence.
    *   Well-designed experiments: The use of a controlled toy setting (ParityMNIST) allows for rigorous ablation studies and validation of the KL hypothesis.
    *   Clear and concise writing: The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, making complex ideas accessible.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Theoretical limitations: The theoretical analysis relies on simplifying assumptions (e.g., binary reward, specific functional forms). It would be useful to explore how the KL-minimization bias holds under more general conditions.
    *   The scope of the empirical results: It may be possible to generalize to other domains of study.
    *   Mechanistic account: The paper explains *why* KL divergence is correlated with forgetting but does not provide a detailed *mechanistic* account of *how* larger KL shifts disrupt prior knowledge.
    * The assumption that the reward is binary may cause an artificial "push" to stick with the original dataset.
    *   The role of negative examples in RL needs to be explored further.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future post-training methods for foundation models, encouraging explicit KL regularization or other techniques that minimize distributional shift. It may also spark further research into the underlying mechanisms of catastrophic forgetting and the trade-offs between adaptation and stability.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:**
The paper provides a clear and compelling case that connects the core concept to previously known principles. This enables the result to be reproduced in different scenarios and potentially build off of to address the weaknesses. While the core mechanism is still not fully understood, this paper shows that it can be a valuable tool in a modern system.

**Score: 8**

Rationale: The paper presents a novel and significant insight into a fundamental problem in continual learning, supported by solid empirical evidence and a plausible theoretical justification. It is well-written and has the potential to influence future research and development in the field. While the theoretical analysis is simplified and a mechanistic account of forgetting is lacking, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### TauGenNet: Plasma-Driven Tau PET Image Synthesis via Text-Guided 3D Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04269v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxin Gong, Se-in Jang, Wei Shao, Yi Su, Kuang Gong
- **Abstract**: Accurate quantification of tau pathology via tau positron emission tomography (PET) scan is crucial for diagnosing and monitoring Alzheimer's disease (AD). However, the high cost and limited availability of tau PET restrict its widespread use. In contrast, structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and plasma-based biomarkers provide non-invasive and widely available complementary information related to brain anatomy and disease progression. In this work, we propose a text-guided 3D diffusion model for 3D tau PET image synthesis, leveraging multimodal conditions from both structural MRI and plasma measurement. Specifically, the textual prompt is from the plasma p-tau217 measurement, which is a key indicator of AD progression, while MRI provides anatomical structure constraints. The proposed framework is trained and evaluated using clinical AV1451 tau PET data from the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) database. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach can generate realistic, clinically meaningful 3D tau PET across a range of disease stages. The proposed framework can help perform tau PET data augmentation under different settings, provide a non-invasive, cost-effective alternative for visualizing tau pathology, and support the simulation of disease progression under varying plasma biomarker levels and cognitive conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
